np.where(np.all(x == X, axis=1))\n    ret = Y[idx, :][0]\n    assert np.size(ret) == x.shape[0]\n    return ret",
    "Experiment Result": "The MetaBO framework is evaluated across various Bayesian Optimization tasks, including hyperparameter optimization (HPO) for AdaBoost and SVM, and global optimization benchmark functions (Branin, Goldstein-Price, Hartmann-3D, Furuta control, and GP samples). The core method involves training a neural acquisition function (NeuralAF) using Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) over a collection of tasks (few-shot learning setup).\n\n**Environment Setup (General, exemplified by AdaBoost HPO):**\n-   **Gym Environment**: `MetaBO-ADABOOST-v0` (and similar for other tasks).\n-   **Dimensionality (D)**: 2 (for AdaBoost, SVM, Branin, GPrice); 3 (for Hartmann-3D, GP samples); 4 (for Furuta).\n-   **Function Type (`f_type`)**: `\"HPO\"` (for AdaBoost, SVM), `\"BRA-var\"`, `\"GPRICE-var\"`, `\"HM3-var\"` (for benchmark functions with variations), `\"GP\"` (for GP samples), `\"Furuta\"` (for control task).\n-   **Optimization Horizon (T)**: Typically 15 (AdaBoost), 20 (SVM), 30 (Branin, GPrice, Hartmann-3D, GP samples). For Furuta, it is variable between T_min=1 and T_max=25.\n-   **Initial Samples (`n_init_samples`)**: 0 for all tasks, meaning no initial design points are given to the GP before the policy starts making decisions.\n-   **Features for Policy (`features`)**: `[\"posterior_mean\", \"posterior_std\", \"timestep\", \"budget\", \"x\"]` is common. For GP samples, `[\"posterior_mean\", \"posterior_std\", \"incumbent\", \"timestep_perc\", \"timestep\", \"budget\"]` is used. For Furuta, `[\"posterior_mean\", \"posterior_std\", \"x\"]` is used.\n-   **GP Surrogate Settings**: The environment internally uses `GPy.models.gp_regression.GPRegression` with an RBF, Matern32, or Matern52 kernel. Kernel hyperparameters (`kernel_lengthscale`, `kernel_variance`, `noise_variance`) are either fixed (e.g., [0.235, 0.578] for Branin, [0.1, 0.1, 0.1, 0.1] for Furuta) or sampled per task (for `f_type=\"GP\"`) or loaded from pre-determined files (for `f_type=\"HPO\"`). A prior mean function (`use_prior_mean_function=True`) is often used, especially for HPO and Furuta tasks.\n-   **Local Acquisition Function Optimization (`local_af_opt`)**: `True` for continuous domains (e.g., Branin, Furuta, GPrice, Hartmann-3D, GP samples), employing multi-start optimization (`N_MS`: 1000-10000, `N_LS`: 1000-4000, `k`: 1-5 top start points). `False` for discrete domains (HPO for AdaBoost, SVM) where a static grid is used.\n-   **Reward Transformation (`reward_transformation`)**: `\"neg_log10\"` (for Branin, GPrice, Hartmann-3D, HPO, GP samples) to maximize negative log regret, or `\"neg_linear\"` (for Furuta) when true maximum is not perfectly known.\n-   **Task Augmentation**: For benchmark functions (Branin, GPrice, Hartmann-3D), functions are varied by random translations (`bound_translation=0.1`) and scalings (`bound_scaling=0.1`) of the input space and objective values, respectively. For HPO and GP sample tasks, `min_regret` (e.g., 1e-5 or 1e-6) is added to ensure logarithmic regret is well-defined.\n\n**Meta-learning Training (PPO Parameters, exemplified by AdaBoost HPO):**\n-   **Optimization Algorithm**: Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO).\n-   **Number of Iterations (`n_iterations`)**: 2000.\n-   **Total Training Steps (`max_steps`)**: `n_iterations * batch_size` (e.g., 2000 * 1200 = 2,400,000 steps).\n-   **Batch Size (`batch_size`)**: 1200.\n-   **Number of Workers (`n_workers`)**: 10 parallel environment runners.\n-   **Minibatch Size (`minibatch_size`)**: `batch_size // 20` (60) or `batch_size // 50` (24 for Furuta).\n-   **Number of Epochs (`n_epochs`)**: 4.\n-   **Learning Rate (`lr`)**: 1e-4.\n-   **PPO Clipping Epsilon (`epsilon`)**: 0.15.\n-   **Value Function Coefficient (`value_coeff`)**: 1.0.\n-   **Entropy Coefficient (`ent_coeff`)**: 0.01.\n-   **Discount Factor (Gamma)**: 0.98.\n-   **GAE Lambda (`lambda`)**: 0.98.\n-   **Loss Type (`loss_type`)**: `\"GAElam\"` (Generalized Advantage Estimation).\n-   **Normalize Advantages (`normalize_advs`)**: True.\n-   **Random Seed (`seed`)**: 0 (for main process and workers).\n-   **Policy Network Options (`policy_options`)**: \n    -   `activations`: `\"relu\"`.\n    -   `arch_spec`: `4 * [200]` (MLP with 4 hidden layers, 200 units each) for AdaBoost, Branin, Furuta, GPrice, Hartmann-3D. For GP samples, `2 * [20]` is used.\n    -   `use_value_network`: True, with `arch_spec_value` typically matching `arch_spec`.\n    -   `t_idx`: -2, `T_idx`: -1 (indices for timestep and budget in feature vector).\n    -   For GP samples, `exclude_t_from_policy=True` and `exclude_T_from_policy=True` are also used, meaning timestep and budget are not fed directly to the policy network."
}{
    "Title": "Meta-learning Hyperparameter Performance Prediction with Neural Processes",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the challenge of inefficient Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) for Sequential Model-based Optimization (SMBO) when individual trials are computationally costly, by leveraging knowledge from historical HPO trials on other datasets. It proposes Transferable Neural Processes (TNP), an end-to-end and efficient HPO algorithm that uniquely integrates three key transfer learning mechanisms: 1) incorporating observations from previous datasets via a dataset-aware attention unit, 2) learning a transferable initialization for the Neural Process (NP) surrogate model's parameters using Model Agnostic Meta-Learning (MAML), and 3) optimizing a set of well-generalized initial hyperparameter configurations for SMBO. The TNP is shown to achieve state-of-the-art performance in significantly fewer trials (at least one order of magnitude less) by effectively measuring dataset similarity without relying on manual meta-features, thereby preventing negative transfer.",
    "Methodology": "TNP utilizes Neural Processes (NPs) as its surrogate model, comprising an encoder, a dataset-aware attention unit, and a decoder. The encoder learns embeddings for each observation. The dataset-aware attention unit aggregates observations from both the current and historical datasets into an order-invariant representation, employing a multi-head attention mechanism where similarity between cross-dataset configurations is modulated by a cosine similarity derived from dataset mean embeddings. This eliminates the need for hand-crafted meta-features. The decoder predicts the mean and variance of the response function for target configurations. For parameter transfer, TNP uses MAML to infer a transferable initialization (global kernel ˜θ) for its parameters, which are then fine-tuned for a target dataset. For initial configuration transfer, a set of well-generalized initial configurations (˜xIj) for SMBO are learned using a hierarchical Bayesian inference approach, similar to MAML, to maximize performance and maintain diversity. The model is trained by maximizing the conditional log-likelihood, involving sampling different response functions (datasets) and splitting observation sets.",
    "Experimental Setup": "TNP was evaluated on two categories of datasets. First, 100 supervised classification datasets from the OpenML platform (fewer than 100,000 instances, no missing values) were used to optimize Logistic Regression (LR) with a 4-dimensional hyperparameter space (learning rate η, l2-regularization r2, batch size B, dropout ratio γ). Second, three computer vision datasets (CIFAR-10, MNIST, SVHN) were used to optimize a three-layer Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) with a 5-dimensional hyperparameter space (learning rate η, batch size B, and hidden units d1, d2, d3 for each layer). Baselines included 11 SMBO-based methods using Expected Improvement (EI), categorized by surrogate model (Random Search, GP, SMAC, DNGO, BOHAMIANN) and knowledge transfer mechanisms (MTGP, EFFICIENT, RGPE, CNP, GP_LI). Evaluation metrics included maximum classification accuracy, average rank over datasets, and scaled average distance to the maximum across iterations. The network setup for TNP's encoder, decoder, and attention embedding function 'g' consisted of two-layer Multilayer Perceptrons (MLPs) with [128, 128] hidden units, pre-trained on 30,000 batches of GP functions. Hyperparameters were optimized on validation sets and performance compared on test sets, with history sets for OpenML generated by running GPs for 100 trials.",
    "Limitations": "The performance of TNP can degrade slightly if the number of historical datasets (M) is too large, as observed with M=50 in experiments, potentially due to the inclusion of more noisy observations. Although the dataset-aware attention mechanism is designed for parallel computation and reduces the number of required SMBO trials, its time complexity still involves summing over all historical observations, O(nX(nI+t+∑M m=1 Tm)).",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research will focus on introducing a 'freeze-thaw' mechanism into the TNP model. This mechanism aims to intelligently allocate limited computational time budgets to the most promising hyperparameter configurations, which the model can quickly identify and estimate through its knowledge transfer capabilities.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Bayesian Optimization for Iterative Learning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the problem of inefficient hyperparameter tuning for deep (reinforcement) learning systems, where iterative training is expensive and traditional Bayesian Optimization (BO) methods ignore valuable intermediate learning progress. The main contributions are: 1) A novel BO approach (BOIL) that effectively exploits the iterative structure of learning algorithms for efficient hyperparameter tuning. 2) The introduction of a training curve compression technique, using a learned Sigmoid function, to transform the entire learning progress into a single numeric score that reflects both training success and stability. 3) A data augmentation strategy that selectively includes intermediate scores from training curves to enhance sample efficiency and avoid issues like ill-conditioned Gaussian Process (GP) covariance matrices. 4) The BOIL framework explicitly balances the benefits of additional training steps against their computational cost. 5) Empirical demonstration that BOIL significantly outperforms existing baselines in identifying optimal hyperparameters in minimal wall-clock time for Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) agents and Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs).",
    "Methodology": "The BOIL framework employs a Gaussian Process (GP) to model the cost-sensitive black-box objective function f(x,t) across a joint space of hyperparameters (x) and training iterations (t). A product kernel, k(x,x') × k(t,t'), is used to induce joint similarities. The training time cost c(x,t) is approximated by a linear regressor. For point acquisition, a modified Expected Improvement (EI) criterion, scaled by the estimated cost (α(x,t)/µc(x,t)), guides the search to balance exploitation, exploration, and cost. A core component is the training curve compression, which uses a Logistic (Sigmoid) function l(u|m0,g0) to convert raw learning curves into a single numeric score. The parameters (m0 and g0) of this Logistic function are learned dynamically by maximizing the GP's log marginal likelihood. To enhance sample efficiency and prevent numerical instability (ill-conditioning of the GP covariance matrix), a selective data augmentation technique is used. This method actively samples a subset of points from the observed training curve based on maximum GP predictive uncertainty, ensuring the natural logarithm of the covariance matrix condition number remains below a predefined threshold (δ=20), and these augmented points are added to the GP's training data.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on an NVIDIA 1080 GTX GPU using the TensorFlow-GPU Python package. All results were averaged over 20 independent runs with different random seeds. The DRL environments used were from OpenAI Gym and Mujoco, specifically CartPole-v0 (with Dueling DQN), InvertedPendulum-v2, and Reacher-v2 (with Advantage Actor Critic agents). DRL implementations were based on OpenAI Baselines. For CNNs, hyperparameter tuning was performed on the SVHN dataset and a subset of CIFAR10. The GP models used square-exponential kernels for both the hyperparameter and iteration components, combined via a product kernel, with kernel parameters optimized by maximizing marginal likelihood. The data augmentation strategy involved a maximum of 15 augmented points per evaluation (M=15) and a condition number threshold of δ=20 for the natural log of the GP covariance matrix. BOIL's performance was compared against Hyperband and Continuous Multi-Task/Fidelity Bayesian Optimization (CM-T/F-BO), which extends discrete multi-task BO to continuous settings.",
    "Limitations": "The paper implies or explicitly mentions several limitations or areas of simplification. The approximation of the cost function c(x,t) by a linear regressor, while robust, might be insufficient for scenarios with more complex cost dependencies, suggesting that a more sophisticated model like a second GP might be needed. The parameters for data augmentation (maximum number of points M=15 and condition number threshold δ=20) are user-defined heuristics, which might not be universally optimal. The scope of the work intentionally omits comparisons of various acquisition functions and kernel choices, which could further influence performance. The optimization of the Logistic curve parameters and GP hyperparameters via multi-start gradient descent could be susceptible to local optima. From a broader impact perspective, the authors acknowledge that increased automation in ML model training contributes to model opacity, necessitating rigorous analysis of training outcomes and the integration of interpretability techniques.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors suggest several promising directions for future research. One key area is extending the applicability of the BOIL framework beyond machine learning to any iterative process that can benefit from exploiting its step-wise structure, such as optimizing manufacturing pipelines. Another direction is to integrate BOIL further into automated pipelines for machine learning model training and deployment. For scenarios with more complex cost functions, exploring the use of a second Gaussian Process or other sophisticated parametric models for c(x,t) could enhance accuracy. Furthermore, while not a direct technical extension of BOIL, the paper implicitly encourages future work in integrating automated tuning with advancements in the interpretability of machine learning models to ensure that complex black-box systems can be rigorously analyzed and understood, safeguarding against potential failures and biases.",

    "Experiment Result": "Gaussian Process Model:\n- Kernel type: Product of RBF kernels for hyperparameters (x) and training iterations (t).\n- Noise delta: 5e-4 (initial and used during optimization).\n- Initial GP hyperparameters: variance=1, lengthscale_x=0.02, lengthscale_t=0.2.\n- GP hyperparameter and Logistic function parameter optimization frequency: Every `2 * D` iterations, where `D` is the problem dimension.\n- Optimization bounds for `lengthscale_x`: [0.03, 0.3].\n- Optimization bounds for `lengthscale_t`: [0.3, 0.6] (calculated as [10 * min_lengthscale_x, 2 * max_lengthscale_x]).\n- Optimization bounds for Logistic function parameters:\n    - `midpoint` (m0): [-2, 3].\n    - `growth` (g0): [0.5, 2].\n\nCost Model:\n- Approximation Method: Linear regression (`sklearn.linear_model.LinearRegression`).\n\nAcquisition Function:\n- Criterion: Modified Expected Improvement (EI).\n- Default name: `ei_mu_max` (uses the maximum of the GP's predictive mean as the incumbent).\n- Scaling: `log(utility) - log(mean_cost)` (or `log(1 + exp(utility)) / log(1 + exp(mean_cost))`, depending on the EI value).\n- A constant offset of 0.1 is added to the predicted cost to avoid zero or negative costs.\n\nTraining Curve Compression:\n- Function type: Logistic (Sigmoid) function `l(u|m0,g0)`.\n- Initial parameters: `midpoint=0.0`, `growth=1.0`.\n- Parameters are learned dynamically by maximizing the GP's log marginal likelihood.\n\nData Augmentation:\n- Technique: Selective sampling of a subset of points from the observed training curve.\n- Selection criterion: Based on maximum GP predictive uncertainty (`pure_exploration` acquisition function applied to a temporary GP).\n- Maximum augmented points per real observation: Up to 10 points.\n- Stopping condition for augmentation: The natural logarithm of the GP covariance matrix condition number (`ln(cond_num)`) remains below a predefined threshold of 15, or if uncertainty is too low.\n- Augmented points are added to the GP's training data.\n\nInitialization:\n- Number of initial points: 3 points.\n- Sampling method: Random uniform within the search space.\n\nReproducibility:\n- Random seeds are set using `np.random.seed(seed)` during initialization."

    "Title": "Efficient Hyperparameter Optimization with Adaptive Fidelity Identification",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the challenge in multi-fidelity Bayesian Optimization (BO) of adaptively determining the appropriate fidelity for each hyperparameter configuration to fit the surrogate model. It proposes FastBO, a multi-fidelity BO method that introduces the novel concepts of 'efficient point' and 'saturation point' for adaptive fidelity identification. FastBO efficiently offers strong performance and rapid convergence. The proposed adaptive fidelity identification strategy is also generalizable, allowing single-fidelity methods to be extended to the multi-fidelity setting.",
    "Methodology": "FastBO adaptively determines the fidelity for each configuration using two key concepts: the 'efficient point' (ei), defined as the resource level beyond which doubling resources yields performance improvement below a small threshold (δ1), and the 'saturation point' (si), where performance stabilizes beyond a given resource level (δ2). The FastBO process involves a warm-up stage for early observations and termination of poor configurations, followed by learning curve estimation. Efficient and saturation points are extracted, and configurations are evaluated up to their efficient point to update the surrogate model. A post-processing stage resumes promising configurations to their saturation points to find optimal ones. This adaptive identification strategy also enables generalization of single-fidelity methods to multi-fidelity settings.",
    "Experimental Setup": "FastBO's performance was evaluated against a suite of existing HPO methods, including random search (RS), standard BO, ASHA, Hyperband, PASHA, A-BOHB, A-CQR, BOHB, DyHPO, and Hyper-Tune. The experiments were conducted on three standard benchmarks: LCBench, NAS-Bench-201, and FCNet. The validation focused on 'anytime performance', demonstrating FastBO's ability to handle various performance metrics and converge rapidly to the global optimum.",
    "Limitations": "The paper is an extended abstract, meaning finer details are omitted. While not explicitly detailed as limitations beyond being an extended abstract, the reliance on predefined small thresholds (δ1 and δ2) for defining efficient and saturation points could be considered a constraint, as their optimal values might be task-dependent. The paper acknowledges challenges that demand future improvements regarding its applicability and scalability to larger search spaces and distributed computing systems, implying current limitations in these areas.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work will focus on refining and expanding FastBO to handle larger search spaces. Additionally, research will explore extending FastBO to distributed computing systems to enhance its applicability and scalability. These directions aim to address current challenges and broaden the method's utility.",

    "Title": "Bayesian Optimization for Iterative Learning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the challenge of expensive hyperparameter tuning in deep (reinforcement) learning systems, which typically involves numerous iterative training steps and often ignores valuable intermediate information. It proposes a Bayesian Optimization (BO) approach, called BOIL, that explicitly exploits the iterative structure of learning algorithms. The key contributions include: 1) An algorithm to optimize the learning curve by compressing the entire training curve into a single numeric score based on training success and stability, rather than relying solely on averaged final performance. 2) An approach to learn the optimal compression curve parameters directly from data and a novel data augmentation technique to enhance sample-efficiency by selectively including intermediate training scores. 3) Empirical demonstration of BOIL's efficiency in tuning hyperparameters for deep reinforcement learning agents and convolutional neural networks, showing superior performance over existing baselines in identifying optimal hyperparameters in minimal wall-clock time.",
    "Methodology": "The proposed BOIL framework models the cost-sensitive objective function `f(x,t)` as a Gaussian Process (GP) over the joint space of hyperparameters `x` and training iterations `t`. A product kernel `k([x,t],[x',t']) = k(x,x') × k(t,t')` with square-exponential components is used. The cost `c(x,t)` is approximated by a linear regressor. Instead of standard final performance metrics, the full learning curve `r(·|x,t)` is compressed into a numeric utility score `y` using a Sigmoid preference function `l(u |m0,g0)`. The parameters `m0` (middle point) and `g0` (growth rate) of this Sigmoid function are learned by maximizing the GP's log marginal likelihood. To improve sample efficiency and GP conditioning, a data augmentation technique is introduced: rather than adding the entire training curve, a subset of `M` augmented points from the observed curve `r(t'|x,t)` are selected based on maximizing GP predictive uncertainty, ensuring the natural log of the covariance matrix condition number stays below a threshold `δ`. The next evaluation point is selected by maximizing a cost-sensitive acquisition function, which modifies the Expected Improvement criterion by dividing it by the predicted cost `µc(x,t)`.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The algorithm's efficiency was evaluated across two main application domains. For Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL), experiments involved tuning a Dueling DQN agent on the CartPole-v0 environment and Advantage Actor Critic (A2C) agents on the InvertedPendulum-v2 and Reacher-v2 environments (using OpenAI Gym and Mujoco). For Deep Learning (DL), hyperparameters of a Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) were tuned on the SVHN and CIFAR10 datasets. All experiments were conducted on an NVIDIA 1080 GTX GPU using the tensorflow-gpu Python package, with results averaged over 20 independent runs. The final performance was assessed by evaluating chosen hyperparameters at the maximum number of iterations. BOIL used square-exponential kernels for its GP, a maximum of 15 augmented points, and a condition number threshold of 20. Baselines for comparison included Hyperband and Continuous Multi-Task/Fidelity BO (CM-T/F-BO), which extends multi-task BO to continuous settings. Ablation studies also compared BOIL to vanilla BO and BO with curve compression (BO-L). The paper's implementation is open-source on GitHub.",
    "Limitations": "The paper highlights a limitation of existing iteration-efficient BO approaches in DRL settings: stopping criteria, such as the exponential decay assumed in Freeze-thaw BO, are often inapplicable due to the unpredictable fluctuations and inherent noisiness of DRL reward curves. While the proposed method aims to overcome this, the DRL's noisy nature is a fundamental challenge. A broader societal concern articulated is that increasing automation in machine learning model training and deployment, to which BOIL contributes, could further remove humans from the modeling process, potentially making it harder to identify critical failures or understand biases. The reliance on a specific threshold for the GP covariance matrix condition number for data augmentation might also present a tuning challenge or lead to sub-optimal augmentation if not carefully chosen.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors suggest that the proposed BOIL framework is not exclusively limited to machine learning algorithms. It can be more generally applied to any iterative process that depends on a set of parameters and exhibits an iterative structure that can be exploited. A concrete example provided is the optimization of manufacturing pipelines, where factory settings could be adjusted to enhance productivity. Implicitly, further work on addressing the broader impact concerns, such as integrating more advanced interpretability methods into automated pipelines or developing mechanisms to ensure human oversight despite increased automation, could also be considered an important future direction.",
    "Experiment Code": "import numpy as np\nfrom bayes_opt.acquisition_functions import AcquisitionFunction, unique_rows\nfrom bayes_opt import GaussianProcess\nfrom bayes_opt import     "Experiment Result": "The BOIL framework models the cost-sensitive objective function `f(x,t)` as a Gaussian Process (GP) over the joint space of hyperparameters `x` and training iterations `t`. A product kernel `k([x,t],[x',t']) = k(x,x') × k(t,t')` with squared-exponential components is used. The initial lengthscales for the `x` and `t` dimensions are `0.02` and `0.2` respectively, with a `noise_delta` of `5e-4`. These kernel hyperparameters are optimized by maximizing the GP's log marginal likelihood. The optimization ranges for `lengthscale_x` and `lengthscale_t` are `[1e-3, 1]` and `[0.01, 2]` respectively.\n\nThe cost `c(x,t)` is approximated by an `sklearn.linear_model.LinearRegression` model, which is fitted using the observed data. The full learning curve `r(·|x,t)` is compressed into a numeric utility score `y` using a Sigmoid preference function `l(u |m0,g0)`. The initial `midpoint (m0)` is `0.0` and `growth (g0)` is `1.0`. These Sigmoid parameters are also learned by maximizing the GP's log marginal likelihood, with optimization ranges `[-2, 3]` for `midpoint` and `[0.5, 2]` for `growth`.\n\nTo improve sample efficiency and GP conditioning, a data augmentation technique is employed. For each observed training curve, a subset of augmented points are selected based on maximizing GP predictive uncertainty (`sqrt(variance)`). The maximum number of augmented points `(M)` is `10` times the maximum number of training iterations (`t_max`). A constraint is applied such that the natural logarithm of the covariance matrix condition number (`np.log(self.gp.cond_num)`) stays below a threshold `δ=15`. This ensures numerical stability of the GP.\n\nThe next evaluation point is selected by maximizing a cost-sensitive acquisition function. This function modifies the Expected Improvement (EI) criterion, specifically `ei_mu_max` (which uses the maximum of the GP's predictive mean as the incumbent best value), by dividing it by the predicted cost `µc(x,t)`. The acquisition value is calculated as `log(utility) - log(mean_cost)` and then minimized using the `L-BFGS-B` method with multiple restarts to find the next point."

    "Title": "Efficient Hyperparameter Optimization with Adaptive Fidelity Identification",
    "Main Contributions": "FastBO addresses the key challenge in multi-fidelity Bayesian Optimization (BO) for Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) and Neural Architecture Search (NAS) by adaptively determining the appropriate fidelity for each hyperparameter configuration to fit the surrogate model. It introduces the novel concepts of \"efficient point\" and \"saturation point\" for each configuration, leading to efficient strong performance. The proposed adaptive fidelity identification strategy also provides a general method to extend any single-fidelity optimization method to a multi-fidelity setting.",
    "Methodology": "FastBO defines two key concepts: an \"efficient point\" (the minimum resource level after which doubling resources yields performance improvement below a small threshold, serving as the fidelity for surrogate model fitting) and a \"saturation point\" (the minimum resource level after which performance stabilizes, indicating approximate final fidelity). The FastBO process involves: 1) a warm-up stage for early observations and termination of poorly performing configurations; 2) estimating the learning curve for each configuration; 3) adaptively extracting its efficient and saturation points; 4) evaluating the configuration up to its efficient point, with results used to update the surrogate model; and 5) a post-processing stage where a small set of promising configurations are evaluated up to their saturation points to identify the optimal configuration. This adaptive fidelity identification is generalizable to extend single-fidelity methods.",
    "Experimental Setup": "FastBO was evaluated against several baseline and state-of-the-art methods, including Random Search (RS), standard BO, ASHA, Hyperband, PASHA, A-BOHB, A-CQR, BOHB, DyHPO, and Hyper-Tune. The performance was assessed on three distinct benchmarks: LCBench, NAS-Bench-201, and FCNet. The validation focused on 'anytime performance,' demonstrating FastBO's ability to gain an advantage earlier and rapidly converge to the global optimum compared to other methods.",
    "Limitations": "The paper primarily highlights the limitations of existing multi-fidelity BO methods (e.g., the assumption that learning curves rarely intersect, fitting separate models for different fidelities, requiring strong assumptions, or using the last observed fidelity which can lead to inaccurate surrogate models). Explicit limitations of FastBO itself are not detailed, though the future research directions implicitly suggest that its current applicability and scalability could be improved for \"larger search spaces and distributed computing systems.\"",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research should focus on refining and expanding FastBO to handle larger search spaces and integrate with distributed computing systems. These extensions aim to further improve its applicability and scalability for more complex and computationally demanding HPO and NAS tasks.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Hyperparameter Optimization through Neural Network Partitioning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes Partitioned Neural Networks, a novel, simple, and efficient method for hyperparameter optimization that operates within a single training run and does not require a separate validation set. Inspired by the marginal likelihood, it partitions both the training data and the neural network model into K data shards and parameter partitions, respectively. Hyperparameters are optimized using an \"out-of-training-sample\" loss on data shards unseen by a subnetwork, thereby inferring generalization capability without a validation set. This approach is demonstrated to be significantly more computationally efficient than alternative marginal likelihood methods for neural networks, scales to larger problems, and is particularly beneficial for challenging scenarios like federated learning.",
    "Methodology": "The method approximates a lower-bound on the marginal likelihood, specifically drawing from the \"learning speed\" perspective. It partitions the neural network's weights into C chunks (w1,...,wC) and the dataset into C chunks (D1,...,DC). Subnetworks w_s^(k) are constructed by concatenating the first k learned parameter partitions (w1,...,wk) with default values (ˆwk+1,...,ˆwC) for subsequent partitions. During partitioned training, parameter updates for wk are performed on data D1:k using subnetwork w_s^(k). Hyperparameter updates are interleaved, optimizing the objective LML, which computes the loss on data from chunk Dk using the subnetwork w_s^(k-1) (i.e., data unseen by w_s^(k-1)). This process effectively measures generalization. The default partitioning scheme involves randomly assigning a fixed proportion of weights in each layer to a partition, with future weights initialized to their starting values or zero.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments cover various tasks: (1) **Input Selection**: A toy task with 15 informative and 15 spurious features, using MLPs. Both non-differentiable model selection and differentiable mask learning (with concrete Bernoulli distribution) were evaluated. (2) **Learning Invariances through Data Augmentations**: Learning affine image augmentations on MNIST, CIFAR10, TinyImagenet, and their rotated variants. Architectures included FixupResNet-8, FixupResNet-14, ResNet-50 (with GroupNorm), and a custom MNIST CNN. (3) **Learning Feature Extractors**: A Wide ResNet-20 on CIFAR10, treating its first two stages as hyperparameters. (4) **Federated Learning**: Optimizing data augmentation parameters and dropout rates on non-i.i.d. splits of MNIST, RotMNIST, CIFAR10, and RotCIFAR10 (with varying dataset sizes). Baselines included Post-hoc diagonal Laplace, Augerino, Differentiable Laplace, Last-layer marginal likelihood, traditional validation set optimization with fine-tuning, FedAvg, and FedAvg + Augerino. Validation primarily used test accuracy and log-likelihood, with sensitivity to partitioning also investigated.",
    "Limitations": "The method requires an additional forward-backward pass for hyperparameter updates, incurring computational costs, although these are significantly lower than competing marginal likelihood methods. Empirically, partitioned networks may also require more training iterations to converge. Partitioning the network inherently constrains model capacity, which can lead to some performance loss compared to an optimally tuned, full, non-partitioned network. Furthermore, the partitioning strategy itself (number of chunks, proportions of data/parameters) introduces an additional hyperparameter that may require tuning for optimal performance. Storing non-zero default weight values for future chunks can also introduce a minor memory overhead.",

In the context of federated learning, exploring different sequential updating schemes for clients is an open direction.     "Title": "Stochastic Marginal Likelihood Gradients using Neural Tangent Kernels",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the scalability limitation of Bayesian hyperparameter optimization in deep learning, where estimating a single hyperparameter gradient requires a full pass through the dataset. It introduces novel lower bounds to the linearized Laplace approximation of the marginal likelihood. These bounds are amenable to stochastic-gradient-based optimization, allowing for a trade-off between estimation accuracy and computational complexity. The work derives these bounds using the function-space form of the linearized Laplace, estimated with the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK), and demonstrates that previously used structured approximations are, in fact, lower bounds. Experimentally, the proposed stochastic estimators significantly accelerate gradient-based hyperparameter optimization (up to 25-fold speed-up) and enable application to larger datasets and models.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves Bayesian model selection by optimizing the marginal likelihood using gradient ascent. The approach relies on the linearized Laplace (LA) approximation to the log marginal likelihood. To enable stochastic gradients, the authors derive lower bounds by utilizing the dual (NTK) form of the log-determinant term in the Laplace approximation. Three key bounds are introduced: 1) Parametric structured lower bounds (Theorem 1) which show that block-diagonal GGN approximations are lower bounds by partitioning parameters. 2) Subset-of-Data Kernel Bounds (Theorem 2) which partition the NTK on subsets of data and outputs, enabling unbiased stochastic estimates and gradients. 3) Parametric Data Subset Bounds (Theorem 3) which demonstrate the equivalence of NTK-based subset bounds to a parametric (GGN-like) variant on data subsets. A further doubly lower bound (Corollary 3.1) combines parametric structured approximations (e.g., KFAC) with subset-of-data processing. Stochastic optimization is performed by sampling mini-batches of data/output indices, and partitioning strategies, such as output-wise or class-wise partitioning, are explored to improve bound tightness and efficiency. An algorithm (Algorithm 1) is provided for stochastic marginal likelihood estimation.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The estimators were validated across various deep learning hyperparameter optimization tasks. For illustrating bound tightness, a small 3-layer Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) with approximately 16,000 parameters was used on random subsets of 1,000 MNIST digits, including rotated MNIST, allowing for analytical computation of full GGN and NTK as references. For benchmarking, a Wide ResNet 16-4 with Fixup initialization was used on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 datasets to optimize layer-wise prior precisions (weight decay) and learn affine invariances. Larger-scale experiments were conducted on TinyImagenet (100,000 data points, 200 classes) using a ResNet-50 (approximately 23 million parameters) with Fixup. Performance was compared against the state-of-the-art full-batch KFAC-Laplace, as well as Augerino and a neural network partitioning method on TinyImagenet. Metrics included negative log marginal likelihood, test log-likelihood, test accuracy, and runtime. Hyperparameters were optimized via Adam/SGD in an interleaved fashion with network weights, with various batch sizes (e.g., 500, 150, 400, 60-70) and output-wise partitioning for efficiency.",
    "Limitations": "The standard log-determinant in the linearized Laplace approximation generally prohibits unbiased stochastic gradients on mini-batches of data, necessitating full dataset passes in previous methods. While the proposed methods mitigate this, using 'too small subset sizes can lead to failure when optimizing them, similar to the diagonal approximation.' The paper notes that Bayesian model selection can lead to overfitting if the number of hyperparameters becomes too large. Furthermore, while KFAC is a common approximation, a theoretical guarantee for its bound as a further approximation to block-diagonal is not provided, though it appears to hold in practice. The proposed class-wise partitioning, while potentially improving bounds, can incur a slightly higher variance in the estimated bound.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could focus on discovering new methods to make the derived bounds tighter, potentially by improving the partitioning strategies of the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK). Another promising direction is to apply these fast stochastic estimators to large-scale Bayesian linear models. Additionally, developing methods to track (anti-)correlation between inputs within the NTK to group them optimally during training, similar to inducing point optimization, could lead to further improvements. The efficiency of stochastic estimators for general differentiable hyperparameter optimization is also highlighted as an exciting future area of research.",
    "Experiment Code": "File Path: data_utils/utils.py\nContent:\nimport numpy as np\nimport torch\nfrom math import ceil\n\n\nclass TensorDataLoader:\n    \"\"\"Combination of torch's DataLoader and data_factor\n        self.n_data = y.size(0)\n        self.batch_size = batch_size\n        self.n_batches = ceil(self.n_data / self.batch_size)\n        self.shuffle = shuffle\n        identity =    def __iter__(self):\n        if self.shuffle:\n            permutation = torch.randperm(self.n_data, device=self.device)\n            self.x = self.x[permutation]\n            self.y = self.y[permutation]\n        self.i_batch = 0\n        return self\n\n    def __next__(self):\n        if self.i_batch >= self.n_batches:\n            raise StopIteration\n\n        start = GroupedSubsetTensorDataLoader(SubsetTensorDataLoader):\n    \n    def __init__(self, x, y, transform=None, transform_y=None, subset_size=500, data_factor=1, detach=False):\n        super().__init__(x, y, transform, transform_y, subset_size, data_factor, detach)\n        self.class_indices = list()\n        for c in torch.unique(self.y):\n            self.class_indices.append((self.y == c).nonzero().squeeze())\n        self.n_classes = len(self.class_indices)\n\n    def __next__(self):\n        if self.i_batch >= self.n_batches:\n            raise StopIteration\n\n        # class   for c in class_order:\n            if n_data_remaining == 0:\n                break\n            local_indices = self.class_indices[c]\n            local_shuffle = torch.randperm(len(local_indices))\n            c_indices = local_indices[local_shuffle][:n_data_remaining]\n            n_data_remaining -= len(c_indices)\n            sod_indices.append(c_indices)\n        sod_indices = torch.cat(sod_indices)\n\n        if self._detach:\n            x = self.transform(self.x[sod_indices]).detach()\n        else:\n            x = all\n    xy_train = [dataset[i] for i in indices]\n    x = torch.stack([e[0] for e in xy_train]).to(device)\n    y = torch.stack([torch.tensor(e[1]) for e in xy_train]).to(device)\n    return x, y\n\nFile Path: dependencies/laplace/laplace/baselaplace.py\nContent:\nfrom abc import ABC, abstractmethod, abstractproperty\nfrom math import sqrt, pi\nimport numpy as np\nimport torch\nfrom torch.nn.utils import parameters_to_vector, vector_to_parameters\nfrom torch.distributions import MultivariateNormal, Dirichlet, Normal\nimport torch.distributed as dist\n\nfrom laplace.utils import (\n    parameters_per_layer, invsqrt_precision, get_nll, validate,\n    diagonal_add_scalar, batch_diagonal_add_scalar\n)\nfrom laplace.matrix import Kron\n\ntry:\n    from laplace.curvature import BackPackGGN as DefaultBackend\nexcept:\n    from laplace.curvature import AsdlGGN as DefaultBackend\n\n\n__all__ = ['BaseLaplace', 'FullLaplace', 'KronLaplace', 'DiagLaplace']\n\n\nclass BaseLaplace(ABC):\n    \"\"\"Baseclass for all Laplace approximations in this library.\n    Subclasses need to specify how the Hessian approximation is initialized,\n    how to add up curvature over training data, how to sample from the\n    Laplace approximation, and how to compute the functional variance.\n\n    A Laplace approximation is represented by a MAP which is given by the\n    `model` parameter and a posterior precision or covariance specifying\n    a Gaussian distribution \\(\\mathcal{N}(\\theta_{MAP}, P^{-1})\\).\n    The goal of this class is to compute the posterior precision \\(P\\)\n    which sums as\n    \\[\n        P = \\sum_{n=1}^N \\nabla^2_\\theta \\log p(\\mathcal{D}_n \\mid \\theta)\n        \\vert_{\\theta_{MAP}} + \\nabla^2_\\theta \\log p(\\theta) \\vert_{\\theta_{MAP}}.\n    \\]\n    Every subclass implements different approximations to the log likelihood Hessians,\n    for example, a diagonal one. The prior is assumed to be Gaussian and therefore we have\n    a simple form for \\(\\nabla^2_\\theta \\log p(\\theta) \\vert_{\\theta_{MAP}} = P_0 \\).\n    In particular, we assume a scalar, layer-wise, or diagonal prior precision so that in\n    all cases \\(P_0 = \\textrm{diag}(p_0)\\) and the structure of \\(p_0\\) can be varied.\n\n    Parameters\n    ----------\n    model : torch.nn.Module\n    likelihood : {'classification', 'regression', 'heteroscedastic_regression'}\n        determines the log likelihood Hessian approximation\n    sigma_noise : torch.Tensor or float, default=1\n        observation noise for the regression setting; must be 1 for classification\n    prior_precision : torch.Tensor or float, default=1\n        prior precision of a Gaussian prior (= weight decay);\n        can be scalar, per-layer, or diagonal in the most general case\n    prior_mean : torch.Tensor or float, default=0\n        prior mean of a Gaussian prior, useful for continual learning\n    temperature : float, default=1\n        temperature of the likelihood; lower temperature leads to more\n        concentrated posterior and vice versa.\n    backend : subclasses of `laplace.curvature.CurvatureInterface`\n        backend for access to curvature/Hessian approximations\n    backend_kwargs : dict, default=None\n        arguments passed to the backend on initialization, for example to\n        set the number of MC samples for stochastic approximations.\n    \"\"\"\n    def __init__(self, model, likelihood, sigma_noise=1., prior_precision=1., prior_mean=0.,\n                 temperature=1., backend=DefaultBackend, backend_kwargs=None, sod=False,\n                 single_output=False, single_output_iid=False):\n        if likelihood not in ['classification', 'regression', 'heteroscedastic_regression']:\n            raise ValueError(f'Invalid likelihood type {likelihood}')\n\n        self.model = model\n        self._device = next(model.parameters()).device\n        # initialize state #\n        # posterior mean/mode\n        self.mean = parameters_to_vector(self.model.parameters()).detach()\n        self.n_params = len(self.mean)\n        self.n_layers = len(list(self.model.parameters()))\n        self.prior_precision = prior_precision\n        self.prior_mean = prior_mean\n        if sigma_noise != 1 and likelihood != 'regression':\n            raise ValueError('Sigma noise != 1 only available for regression.')\n        self.likelihood = likelihood\n        self.sigma_noise = sigma_noise\n        self.temperature = temperature\n        self._backend = None\n        self._backend_cls = backend\n        self._backend_kwargs = dict() if backend_kwargs is None else backend_kwargs\n        self.H = None\n        self.sod = sod\n        self.single_output = single_output\n        self.single_output_iid = single_output_iid\n\n        # log likelihood = g(loss)\n        self.loss = 0.\n        self.n_outputs = None\n        self.n_data = None\n\n    @property\n    def backend(self):\n        if self._backend is None:\n            self._backend = self._backend_cls(self.model, self.likelihood,\n                                              **self._backend_kwargs)\n        return self._backend\n\n    @abstractmethod\n    def _init_H(self):\n        self.H = None\n        self.loss = 0\n        self.n_data_seen = 0\n\n    @abstractmethod\n    def _curv_closure(self, X, y, N):\n        pass\n\n    def _check_fit(self):\n        if self.H is None:\n            raise AttributeError('Laplace not fitted. Run fit() first.')\n\n    def fit(self, train_loader, **kwargs):\n        \"\"\"Fit the local Laplace approximation at the parameters of the model.\n\n        Parameters\n        ----------\n        train_loader : torch.data.utils.DataLoader\n            each iterate is a training batch (X, y);\n            `train_loader.dataset` needs to be set to access \\(N\\), size of the data set\n        \"\"\"\n        self._init_H()\n\n        self.model.eval()\n\n        if getattr(self.model, 'output_size', None) is None:\n            with torch.no_grad():\n                X, _ = next(iter(train_loader))\n                self.n_outputs = self.model(X[:1].to(self._device)).shape[-1]\n            setattr(self.model, 'output_size', self.n_outputs)\n        else:\n            self.n_outputs = getattr(self.model, 'output_size')\n\n        N = len(train_loader.dataset)\n        for X, y in train_loader:\n            self.model.zero_grad()\n            X, y = X.to(self._device), y.to(self._device)\n            loss_batch, H_batch = self._curv_closure(X, y, len(y) if self.sod else N)\n            self.loss += loss_batch\n            self.H += H_batch\n            self.n_data_seen += len(y)\n\n        self.n_data = N\n\n    def fit_batch(self, x, y, N):\n        self._init_H()\n        self.n_data = N\n        self.n_outputs = getattr(self.model, 'output_size')\n        self.model.zero_grad()\n        loss_batch, H_batch = self._curv_closure(x, y, len(y))\n        self.loss += loss_batch\n        self.H += H_batch\n        self.n_data_seen += len(y)\n\n    def fit_distributed(self, train_loader, n_steps_sod=1, **kwargs):\n        \"\"\"Fit the local Laplace approximation at the parameters of the model.\n\n        Parameters\n        ----------\n        train_loader : torch.data.utils.DataLoader\n            each iterate is a training batch (X, y);\n            `train_loader.dataset` needs to be set to access \\(N\\), size of the data set\n        n_steps_sod : int\n            number of steps to take to tighten the bound in the distributed setting\n        \"\"\"\n        self._init_H()\n        self.model.eval()\n        self.n_outputs = getattr(self.model, 'output_size')\n        factor = dist.get_world_size()\n        N = len(train_loader.dataset)\n        i = 0\n        for X, y in train_loader:\n            self.model.zero_grad()\n            X, y = X.to(self._device, non_blocking=True), y.to(self._device, non_blocking=True)\n            M = factor * len(y) * n_steps_sod  # total samples\n            loss_batch, H_batch = self._curv_closure(X, y, M if self.sod else N)\n            self.loss += loss_batch.detach()\n            self.H += H_batch\n            self.n_data_seen += len(y)\n            i += 1\n            if self.sod and (i == n_steps_sod):\n                break\n\n        self.n_data = N\n        self.all_reduce()\n\n    def all_reduce(self):\n        total = torch.tensor(\n            [self.n_data_seen, self.loss.item()], dtype=torch.float32, device=self._device\n        )\n        dist.all_reduce(total, dist.ReduceOp.SUM, async_op=False)\n        self.n_data_seen, self.loss = total.tolist()\n\n    def log_marginal_likelihood(self, prior_precision=None, sigma_noise=None):\n        \"\"\"Compute the Laplace approximation to the log marginal likelihood subject\n        to specific Hessian approximations that subclasses implement.\n        Requires that the Laplace approximation has been fit before.\n        The resulting torch.Tensor is differentiable in `prior_precision` and\n        `sigma_noise` if these have gradients enabled.\n        By passing `prior_precision` or `sigma_noise`, the current value is\n        overwritten. This is useful for iterating on the log marginal likelihood.\n\n        Parameters\n        ----------\n        prior_precision : torch.Tensor, optional\n            prior precision if should be changed from current `prior_precision` value\n        sigma_noise : [type], optional\n            observation noise standard deviation if should be changed\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        log_marglik : torch.Tensor\n        \"\"\"\n        # make sure we can differentiate wrt prior and sigma_noise for regression\n        self._check_fit()\n\n        # update prior precision (useful when iterating on marglik)\n        if prior_precision is not None:\n            self.prior_precision = prior_precision\n\n        # update sigma_noise (useful when iterating on marglik)\n        if sigma_noise is not None:\n            if self.likelihood != 'regression':\n                raise ValueError('Can only change sigma_noise for regression.')\n            self.sigma_noise = sigma_noise\n\n        return self.log_likelihood - 0.5 * (self.log_det_ratio + self.scatter)\n\n    @property\n    def log_likelihood(self):\n        \"\"\"Compute log likelihood on the training data after `.fit()` has been called.\n        The log likelihood is computed on-demand based on the loss and, for example,\n        the observation noise which makes it differentiable in the latter for\n        iterative updates.\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        log_likelihood : torch.Tensor\n        \"\"\"\n        self._check_fit()\n\n        sod_factor = 1.0 if not self.sod else self.n_data / self.n_data_seen\n        factor = - self._H_factor\n        if self.likelihood == 'regression':\n            # loss used is just MSE, need to add normalizer for gaussian likelihood\n            c_factor = (self.n_data if not self.sod else self.n_data_seen) * self.n_outputs / self.temperature\n            c = c_factor * torch.log(self.sigma_noise * sqrt(2 * pi))\n            return sod_factor * (factor * self.loss - c)\n        else:\n            # for classification Xent == log Cat\n            return sod_factor * factor * self.loss\n\n    def __call__(self, x, pred_type='glm', link_approx='probit', n_samples=100, het_approx='natural'):\n        \"\"\"Compute the posterior predictive on input data `X`.\n\n        Parameters\n        ----------\n        x : torch.Tensor\n            `(batch_size, input_shape)`\n\n        pred_type : {'glm', 'nn'}, default='glm'\n            type of posterior predictive, linearized GLM predictive or neural\n            network sampling predictive. The GLM predictive is consistent with\n            the curvature approximations used here.\n\n        link_approx : {'mc', 'probit', 'bridge', 'mcparam'}\n            how to approximate the classification link function for the `'glm'`.\n            For `pred_type='nn'`, only 'mc' is possible.\n\n        n_samples : int\n            number of samples for `link_approx='mc'`.\n\n        het_approx : {'natural', 'mean', 'mc'}\n            decides whether to linearize first natural parameter, mean, or sample.\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        predictive: torch.Tensor or Tuple[torch.Tensor]\n            For `likelihood='classification'`, a torch.Tensor is returned with\n            a distribution over classes (similar to a Softmax).\n            For `likelihood='regression'`, a tuple of torch.Tensor is returned\n            with the mean and the predictive variance.\n        \"\"\"\n        self._check_fit()\n\n        if pred_type not in ['glm', 'nn']:\n            raise ValueError('Only glm and nn supported as prediction types.')\n\n        if link_approx not in ['mc', 'probit', 'bridge', 'mcparam']:\n            raise ValueError(f'Unsupported link approximation {link_approx}.')\n\n        if pred_type == 'glm':\n            # regression\n            if self.likelihood == 'regression':\n                return self._glm_predictive_distribution(x)\n            elif self.likelihood == 'heteroscedastic_regression':\n                if het_approx == 'natural':\n                    return self._glm_het_natural_predictive_distribution(x)\n                elif het_approx == 'mean':\n                    return self._glm_het_mean_predictive_distribution(x)\n                elif het_approx == 'mc':\n                    # sample and estimate with mixture of gaussians\n                    f_mu, f_var = self._glm_predictive_distribution(x)\n                    try:\n                        dist = MultivariateNormal(f_mu, f_var)\n                    except:\n                        dist = Normal(f_mu, torch.diagonal(f_var, dim1=1, dim2=2).sqrt())\n                    samples = dist.sample((n_samples,))  # (n_samples, batch_size, n_outputs)\n                    mu_samples = - samples[:, :, 0] / (2 * samples[:, :, 1])\n                    var_samples = - 0.5 / samples[:, :, 1]\n                    mu = mu_samples.mean(dim=0)\n                    var = (mu_samples.square().mean(dim=0) + var_samples.mean(dim=0)) - mu.square()\n                    return mu, var\n                else:\n                    raise ValueError('')\n            # classification\n            if link_approx == 'mcparam':\n                Js, f_mu = self.backend.jacobians(x)\n                samples = self.sample(n_samples)\n                f_offset = f_mu - Js @ self.mean\n                f_sample_onset = Js @ self.sample(n_samples).T\n                f_samples = f_offset.unsqueeze(-1) + f_sample_onset\n                return torch.softmax(f_samples, dim=1).mean(dim=-1)\n            elif link_approx == 'mc':\n                f_mu, f_var = self._glm_predictive_distribution(x)\n                try:\n                    dist = MultivariateNormal(f_mu, f_var)\n                except:\n                    dist = Normal(f_mu, torch.diagonal(f_var, dim1=1, dim2=2).sqrt())\n                return torch.softmax(dist.sample((n_samples,)), dim=-1).mean(dim=0)\n            elif link_approx == 'probit':\n                f_mu, f_var = self._glm_predictive_distribution(x)\n                kappa = 1 / torch.sqrt(1. + np.pi / 8 * f_var.diagonal(dim1=1, dim2=2))\n                return torch.softmax(kappa * f_mu, dim=-1)\n            elif link_approx == 'bridge':\n                f_mu, f_var = self._glm_predictive_distribution(x)\n                _, K = f_mu.size(0), f_mu.size(-1)\n                f_var_diag = torch.diagonal(f_var, dim1=1, dim2=2)\n                sum_exp = torch.sum(torch.exp(-f_mu), dim=1).unsqueeze(-1)\n                alpha = 1/f_var_diag * (1 - 2/K + torch.exp(f_mu)/(K**2) * sum_exp)\n                dist = Dirichlet(alpha)\n                return torch.nan_to_num(dist.mean, nan=1.0)\n        else:\n            samples = self._nn_predictive_samples(x, n_samples)\n            if self.likelihood == 'regression':\n                return samples.mean(dim=0), samples.var(dim=0)\n            return samples.mean(dim=0)\n\n    def predictive(self, x, pred_type='glm', link_approx='mc', n_samples=100):\n        return self(x, pred_type, link_approx, n_samples)\n\n    def predictive_samples(self, x, pred_type='glm', n_samples=100):\n        \"\"\"Sample from the posterior predictive on input data `x`.\n        Can be used, for example, for Thompson sampling.\n\n        Parameters\n        ----------\n        x : torch.Tensor\n            input data `(batch_size, input_shape)`\n\n        pred_type : {'glm', 'nn'}, default='glm'\n            type of posterior predictive, linearized GLM predictive or neural\n            network sampling predictive. The GLM predictive is consistent with\n            the curvature approximations used here.\n\n        n_samples : int\n            number of samples\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        samples : torch.Tensor\n            samples `(n_samples, batch_size, output_shape)`\n        \"\"\"\n        self._check_fit()\n\n        if pred_type not in ['glm', 'nn']:\n            raise ValueError('Only glm and nn supported as prediction types.')\n\n        if pred_type == 'glm':\n            f_mu, f_var = self._glm_predictive_distribution(x)\n            assert f_var.shape == torch.Size([f_mu.shape[0], f_mu.shape[1], f_mu.shape[1]])\n            try:\n                dist = MultivariateNormal(f_mu, f_var)\n            except:\n                dist = Normal(f_mu, torch.diagonal(f_var, dim1=1, dim2=2).sqrt())\n            samples = dist.sample((n_samples,))\n            if self.likelihood == 'classification':\n                return torch.softmax(samples, dim=-1)\n            return samples\n\n        else:  # 'nn'\n            return self._nn_predictive_samples(x, n_samples)\n\n    @torch.enable_grad()\n    def _glm_predictive_distribution(self, X):\n        Js, f_mu = self.backend.jacobians(X)\n        f_var = self.functional_variance(Js)\n        return f_mu.detach(), f_var.detach()\n\n    @torch.enable_grad()\n    def _glm_het_natural_predictive_distribution(self, X):\n        Jseta1, f = self.backend.single_jacobians(X, 0)\n        y_var = - 0.5 / f[:, 1]\n        Jsmean = Jseta1 * y_var.unsqueeze(-1)\n        f_var = self.functional_variance(Jsmean.unsqueeze(1)).squeeze()\n        f_mean = f[:, 0] * y_var\n        return f_mean.detach(), f_var.detach(), y_var.detach()\n\n    @torch.enable_grad()\n    def _glm_het_mean_predictive_distribution(self, X):\n        Jsmean, f = self.backend.mean_jacobians(X)\n        y_var = - 0.5 / f[:, 1]\n        f_var = self.functional_variance(Jsmean.unsqueeze(1)).squeeze()\n        f_mean = f[:, 0] * y_var\n        return f_mean.detach(), f_var.detach(), y_var.detach()\n\n    def _nn_predictive_samples(self, X, n_samples=100):\n        fs = list()\n        for sample in self.sample(n_samples):\n            vector_to_parameters(sample, self.model.parameters())\n            fs.append(self.model(X.to(self._device)).detach())\n        vector_to_parameters(self.mean, self.model.parameters())\n        fs = torch.stack(fs)\n        if self.likelihood == 'classification':\n            fs = torch.softmax(fs, dim=-1)\n        return fs\n\n    @abstractmethod\n    def functional_variance(self, Jacs):\n        \"\"\"Compute functional variance for the `'glm'` predictive:\n        `f_var[i] = Jacs[i] @ P.inv() @ Jacs[i].T`, which is a output x output\n        predictive covariance matrix.\n        Mathematically, we have for a single Jacobian\n        \\(\\mathcal{J} = \\nabla_\\theta f(x;\\theta)\\vert_{\\theta_{MAP}}\\*)\n        the output covariance matrix\n        \\( \\mathcal{J} P^{-1} \\mathcal{J}^T \\).\n\n        Parameters\n        ----------\n        Jacs : torch.Tensor\n            Jacobians of model output wrt parameters\n            `(batch, outputs, parameters)`\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        f_var : torch.Tensor\n            output covariance `(batch, outputs, outputs)`\n        \"\"\"\n        pass\n\n    def _check_jacobians(self, Js):\n        if not isinstance(Js, torch.Tensor):\n            raise ValueError('Jacobians have to be torch.Tensor.')\n        if not Js.device == self._device:\n            raise ValueError('Jacobians need to be on the same device as Laplace.')\n        m, k, p = Js.size()\n        if p != self.n_params:\n            raise ValueError('Invalid Jacobians shape for Laplace posterior approx.')\n\n    @abstractmethod\n    def sample(self, n_samples=100):\n        \"\"\"Sample from the Laplace posterior approximation, i.e.,\n        \\( \\theta \\sim \\mathcal{N}(\\theta_{MAP}, P^{-1})\\).\"\"\"\n        pass\n\n    @property\n    def scatter(self):\n        \"\"\"Computes the _scatter_, a term of the log marginal likelihood that\n        corresponds to L-2 regularization:\n        `scatter` = \\((\\theta_{MAP} - \\mu_0)^{T} P_0 (\\theta_{MAP} - \\mu_0) \\).\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        [type]\n            [description]\n        \"\"\"\n        delta = (self.mean - self.prior_mean)\n        return (delta * self.prior_precision_diag) @ delta\n\n    @property\n    def log_det_prior_precision(self):\n        \"\"\"Compute log determinant of the prior precision\n        \\(\\log \\det P_0\\)\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        log_det : torch.Tensor\n        \"\"\"\n        return self.prior_precision_diag.log().sum()\n\n    @abstractproperty\n    def log_det_posterior_precision(self):\n        \"\"\"Compute log determinant of the posterior precision\n        \\(\\log \\det P\\) which depends on the subclasses structure\n        used for the Hessian approximation.\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        log_det : torch.Tensor\n        \"\"\"\n        pass\n\n    @property\n    def log_det_ratio(self):\n        \"\"\"Compute the log determinant ratio, a part of the log marginal likelihood.\n        \\[\n            \\log \\frac{\\det P}{\\det P_0} = \\log \\det P - \\log \\det P_0\n        \\]\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        log_det_ratio : torch.Tensor\n        \"\"\"\n        sod_factor = 1.0 if not self.sod else self.n_data / self.n_data_seen\n        if self.single_output:\n            sod_factor *= self.n_outputs\n        return sod_factor * (self.log_det_posterior_precision - self.log_det_prior_precision)\n\n    @property\n    def prior_structure(self):\n        if len(self.prior_precision) == 1:\n            return 'scalar'\n        elif len(self.prior_precision) == self.n_params:\n            return 'diagonal'\n        elif len(self.prior_precision) == self.n_layers:\n            return 'layerwise'\n\n    @property\n    def prior_precision_diag(self):\n        \"\"\"Obtain the diagonal prior precision \\(p_0\\) constructed from either\n        a scalar, layer-wise, or diagonal prior precision.\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        prior_precision_diag : torch.Tensor\n        \"\"\"\n        if len(self.prior_precision) == 1:  # scalar\n            return self.prior_precision * torch.ones_like(self.mean, device=self.prior_precision.device)\n\n        elif len(self.prior_precision) == self.n_params:  # diagonal\n            return self.prior_precision\n\n        elif len(self.prior_precision) == self.n_layers:  # per layer\n            n_params_per_layer = parameters_per_layer(self.model)\n            return torch.cat([prior * torch.ones(n_params, device=self.prior_precision.device) for prior, n_params\n                              in zip(self.prior_precision, n_params_per_layer)])\n\n        else:\n            raise ValueError('Mismatch of prior and model. Diagonal, scalar, or per-layer prior.')\n\n    @property\n    def prior_mean(self):\n        return self._prior_mean\n\n    @prior_mean.setter\n    def prior_mean(self, prior_mean):\n        if np.isscalar(prior_mean) and np.isreal(prior_mean):\n            self._prior_mean = torch.tensor(prior_mean, device=self._device)\n        elif torch.is_tensor(prior_mean):\n            if prior_mean.ndim == 0:\n                self._prior_mean = prior_mean.reshape(-1).to(self._device)\n            elif prior_mean.ndim == 1:\n                if not len(prior_mean) in [1, self.n_params]:\n                    raise ValueError('Invalid length of prior mean.')\n                self._prior_mean = prior_mean\n            else:\n                raise ValueError('Prior mean has too many dimensions!')\n        else:\n            raise ValueError('Invalid argument type of prior mean.')\n\n    @property\n    def prior_precision(self):\n        return self._prior_precision\n\n    @prior_precision.setter\n    def prior_precision(self, prior_precision):\n        self._posterior_scale = None\n        if np.isscalar(prior_precision) and np.isreal(prior_precision):\n            self._prior_precision = torch.tensor([prior_precision], device=self._device)\n        elif torch.is_tensor(prior_precision):\n            if prior_precision.ndim == 0:\n                # make dimensional\n                self._prior_precision = prior_precision.reshape(-1).to(self._device)\n            elif prior_precision.ndim == 1:\n                if len(prior_precision) not in [1, self.n_layers, self.n_params]:\n                    raise ValueError('Length of prior precision does not align with architecture.')\n                self._prior_precision = prior_precision.to(self._device)\n            else:\n                raise ValueError('Prior precision needs to be at most one-dimensional tensor.')\n        else:\n            raise ValueError('Prior precision either scalar or torch.Tensor up to 1-dim.')\n\n    def optimize_prior_precision(self, method='marglik', n_steps=100, lr=1e-1,\n                                 init_prior_prec=1., val_loader=None, loss=get_nll,\n                                 log_prior_prec_min=-4, log_prior_prec_max=4, grid_size=100,\n                                 pred_type='glm', link_approx='probit', n_samples=100,\n                                 verbose=False):\n        \"\"\"Optimize the prior precision post-hoc using the `method`\n        specified by the user.\n\n        Parameters\n        ----------\n        method : {'marglik', 'CV'}, default='marglik'\n            specifies how the prior precision should be optimized.\n        n_steps : int, default=100\n            the number of gradient descent steps to take.\n        lr : float, default=1e-1\n            the learning rate to use for gradient descent.\n        init_prior_prec : float, default=1.0\n            initial prior precision before the first optimization step.\n        val_loader : torch.data.utils.DataLoader, default=None\n            DataLoader for the validation set; each iterate is a training batch (X, y).\n        loss : callable, default=get_nll\n            loss function to use for CV.\n        log_prior_prec_min : float, default=-4\n            lower bound of gridsearch interval for CV.\n        log_prior_prec_max : float, default=4\n            upper bound of gridsearch interval for CV.\n        grid_size : int, default=100\n            number of values to consider inside the gridsearch interval for CV.\n        pred_type : {'glm', 'nn'}, default='glm'\n            type of posterior predictive, linearized GLM predictive or neural\n            network sampling predictive. The GLM predictive is consistent with\n            the curvature approximations used here.\n        link_approx : {'mc', 'probit', 'bridge'}, default='probit'\n            how to approximate the classification link function for the `'glm'`.\n            For `pred_type='nn'`, only `'mc'` is possible.\n        n_samples : int, default=100\n            number of samples for `link_approx='mc'`.\n        verbose : bool, default=False\n            if true, the optimized prior precision will be printed\n            (can be a large tensor if the prior has a diagonal covariance).\n        \"\"\"\n        if method == 'marglik':\n            self.prior_precision = init_prior_prec\n            log_prior_prec = self.prior_precision.log()\n            log_prior_prec.requires_grad = True\n            optimizer = torch.optim.Adam([log_prior_prec], lr=lr)\n            for _ in range(n_steps):\n                optimizer.zero_grad()\n                prior_prec = log_prior_prec.exp()\n                neg_log_marglik = -self.log_marginal_likelihood(prior_precision=prior_prec)\n                neg_log_marglik.backward()\n                optimizer.step()\n            self.prior_precision = log_prior_prec.detach().exp()\n        elif method == 'CV':\n            if val_loader is None:\n                raise ValueError('CV requires a validation set DataLoader')\n            interval = torch.logspace(\n                log_prior_prec_min, log_prior_prec_max, grid_size\n            )\n            self.prior_precision = self._gridsearch(\n                loss, interval, val_loader, pred_type=pred_type,\n                link_approx=link_approx, n_samples=n_samples\n            )\n        else:\n            raise ValueError('For now only marglik and CV is implemented.')\n        if verbose:\n            print(f'Optimized prior precision is {self.prior_precision}.')\n\n    def _gridsearch(self, loss, interval, val_loader, pred_type='glm',\n                    link_approx='probit', n_samples=100):\n        results = list()\n        prior_precs = list()\n        for prior_prec in interval:\n            self.prior_precision = prior_prec\n            try:\n                out_dist, targets = validate(\n                    self, val_loader, pred_type=pred_type,\n                    link_approx=link_approx, n_samples=n_samples\n                )\n                result = loss(out_dist, targets)\n            except RuntimeError:\n                result = np.inf\n            results.append(result)\n            prior_precs.append(prior_prec)\n        return prior_precs[np.argmin(results)]\n\n    @property\n    def sigma_noise(self):\n        return self._sigma_noise\n\n    @sigma_noise.setter\n    def sigma_noise(self, sigma_noise):\n        self._posterior_scale = None\n        if np.isscalar(sigma_noise) and np.isreal(sigma_noise):\n            self._sigma_noise = torch.tensor(sigma_noise, device=self._device)\n        elif torch.is_tensor(sigma_noise):\n            if sigma_noise.ndim == 0:\n                self._sigma_noise = sigma_noise.to(self._device)\n            elif sigma_noise.ndim == 1:\n                if           raise ValueError('Sigma noise needs to be scalar or 1-dimensional.')\n        else:\n            raise ValueError('Invalid type: sigma noise needs to be torch.Tensor or scalar.')\n\n    @property\n    def _H_factor(self):\n        sigma2 = self.sigma_noise.square()\n        return 1 / sigma2 / self.temperature\n\n    @abstractproperty\n    def posterior_precision(self):\n        \"\"\"Compute or return the posterior precision \\(P\\).\"\"\"\n        pass\n\n\nclass FunctionalLaplace(BaseLaplace):\n\n    def __init__(self, model, likelihood, sigma_noise=1, prior_precision=1, super().__init__(model, likelihood, sigma_noise=sigma_noise,\n                         prior_precision=prior_precision, sod=sod,\n                         prior_mean=prior_mean, ValueError('Single output assumes independence for splitting.')\n\n    def _kernel_closure(self, X, y):\n        if self.independent:\n            if self.single_output:\n                if self.single_output_iid:\n                    random_ix = torch.randint(self.n_outputs, (len(y),), device=X.device)\n                else:\n                    random_ix = torch.randint(self.n_outputs, ())\n                return self.backend.single_kernel(X, y, self.prior_precision, output_ix=random_ix)\n            else:\n                return self.backend.indep_kernel(X, y, self._init_H()\n        for X, y in train_loader:\n            self.fit_batch(X, y, len(train_loader.dataset))\n\n    def fit_batch(self, X, y, N):\n        self.n_data = N\n        if self.H is self.model(X[:1].to(self._device)).shape[-1]\n            setattr(self.model, 'output_size', self.n_outputs)\n        else:\n            self.n_outputs = getattr(self.model, 'output_size')\n\n                   # H_kernel n x n\n                    log_det_ratio += self.n_outputs * torch.logdet(\n                        diagonal_add_scalar(self._H_factor * H_kernel, 1.0)\n                    )\n                else:\n                    # H_kernel c x n x n\n                    log_det_ratio += torch.logdet(\n                        batch_diagonal_add_scalar(self._H_factor * H_kernel, H_kernel, 1.0)\n                )\n        return self.n_data / self.n_data_seen * log_det_ratio\n\n    def _curv_closure(self, X, y, N):\n        return super()._curv_closure(X, y, N)\n\n    def FullLaplace(BaseLaplace):\n    \"\"\"Laplace approximation with full, i.e., dense, log likelihood Hessian approximation\n    and hence posterior precision. Based on the chosen `backend` parameter, the full\n    approximation can be, for example, a generalized Gauss-Newton matrix.\n    Mathematically, we have \\(P \\in \\mathbb{R}^{P \\times P}\\).\n    See `BaseLaplace` for the full interface.\n    \"\"\"\n    # key to map to correct subclass of BaseLaplace, (subset of weights, Hessian structure)\n    _key = ('all', 'full')\n\n    def __init__(self, torch.randint(self.n_outputs, (len(y),), device=X.device)\n            else:\n                random_ix = torch.randint(self.n_outputs, ())\n            return self.backend.single_full(X, y, random_ix, N=N)\n        return self.backend.full(X, y, N=N)\n\n    def _compute_scale(self):\n        self._posterior_scale = invsqrt_precision(self.posterior_precision)\n\n    @property\n    def posterior_scale(self):\n        \"\"\"Posterior scale (square root of the covariance), i.e.,\n        \\(P^{-\\frac{1}{2}}\\*).\n\n        Returns\n        -------\n        scale : torch.tensor\n            `(parameters, parameters)`\n        \"\"\"\n        if self._posterior_scale is None:\n            self._compute_scale()\n        return self._posterior_scale\n\n    @property\n    def posterior_covariance(self):\n        \"\"\"Posterior covariance, i.e., \\(P^{-1}\\).\"\"\"\n        scale = self.posterior_scale\n        return scale @ scale.T\n\n    @property\n    def posterior_precision(self):\n        \"\"\"Posterior precision \\(P\\).\"\"\"\n        self._check_fit()\n        return self._H_factor * self.H + torch.diag(self.prior_precision_diag)\n\n    @property\n    def log_det_posterior_precision(self):\n        return self.posterior_precision.logdet()\n\n    def functional_variance(self, Js):\n        return torch.einsum('ncp,pq,nkq->nck', Js, dist.sample((n_samples,))\n\n\nclass BlockDiagLaplace(FullLaplace):\n    \"\"\"Naive Blockdiagonal Laplace approximation for testing and development purposes.\"\"\"\n\n    def fit(self, train_loader, samples.reshape(n_samples, self.n_params)\n\n    @BaseLaplace.prior_precision.setter\n    def prior_precision(self, prior_precision):\n        # Extend setter from Laplace to restrict prior precision
