    "Title": "PASHA: Efficient HPO and NAS with Progressive Resource Allocation",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the high computational cost of Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) and Neural Architecture Search (NAS), particularly when tuning models on large datasets, even with existing multi-fidelity methods like ASHA. The main contribution is the proposal of PASHA (Progressive ASHA), an extension of ASHA that dynamically allocates maximum resources for the tuning procedure based on need. PASHA aims to identify well-performing hyperparameter configurations and architectures using significantly fewer computational resources than ASHA, thereby reducing the entry barrier for practitioners. Empirical evaluations demonstrate substantial speedups without sacrificing performance, and the approach is shown to be combinable with sample-efficient strategies like Bayesian Optimization.",
    "Methodology": "PASHA is an extension of ASHA, inspired by the 'doubling trick,' and operates under the assumption that significant changes in configuration rankings primarily occur in the early stages of training. The algorithm begins with a small initial maximum resource allocation and progressively increases it only if the ranking of configurations in the top two rungs (promotion rounds) has not stabilized. A key innovation is 'soft ranking,' where configurations are deemed equivalent if their performance difference is below an automatically estimated threshold, epsilon (ϵ). The value of ϵ is dynamically estimated by measuring noise in rankings, specifically by identifying pairs of configurations that repeatedly swap ranks across different resource levels, and is set as the N-th percentile of performance differences for such 'criss-crossing' pairs. PASHA uses a user-defined minimum resource 'r' and a reduction factor 'η' (defaulting to 3) for pruning, and includes a maximum resource 'R' as a safety net. The implementation is based on the Syne Tune library.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The evaluation of PASHA focused on achieving significantly faster tuning times and on-par predictive performance compared to ASHA. Experiments involved two phases: 1) running the HPO/NAS optimizer until 256 candidate configurations were evaluated, and 2) retraining the best-identified configuration from scratch. All models were retrained using only the training set for consistency with benchmarks. Runtime reported is for the HPO phase only. Experiments were averaged over multiple repetitions (e.g., 15 for NAS, 5 for HPO). Key parameters included N=90th percentile for ϵ estimation and 4 parallel workers. Baselines included ASHA, 'one-epoch baseline,' and 'random baseline.'\n\n**NAS Experiments:** Utilized NASBench201 on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet16-120 datasets, with a minimum resource 'r'=1 epoch and maximum 'R'=200 epochs.\n\n**HPO Experiments:** Used the PD1 benchmark, specifically WMT15 German-English (4.5M examples) with xformer models and ImageNet (1.3M examples) with ResNet50. Hyperparameters optimized included learning rate, momentum, polynomial learning rate decay power, and decay steps fraction.\n\n**Additional Analyses:** Included testing different reduction factors (η=2, η=4), combining PASHA with Bayesian Optimization (MOBSTER), and evaluating various alternative ranking functions (direct ranking, fixed/heuristic ϵ soft ranking, RBO, RRR, ARRR). Further analysis covered performance on LCBench (a benchmark with limited epochs), the impact of variable maximum resources, and the evolution of learning curves and ϵ values.",
    "Limitations": "PASHA's benefits in terms of speedup are reduced when the number of available rungs is small, such as in benchmarks like LCBench where the maximum resources per configuration are limited (e.g., 50 epochs). In such scenarios, there are fewer opportunities for PASHA to interrupt the tuning process early. Public benchmarks often fix minimum resources to one epoch, which can be too coarse for effective early stopping if the total training duration is short. The authors recommend having a maximum amount of resources at least 100 times larger than the minimum (for η=3) for PASHA to yield significant speedups. This limitation can be mitigated by defining resource levels with higher granularity (e.g., in terms of gradient updates instead of epochs).",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could investigate how the definition of rungs and resource levels impacts the decisions of multi-fidelity algorithms, including PASHA. This would involve a deeper understanding of how these factors influence the early stopping mechanism. Additionally, the authors suggest exploring combinations of PASHA with transfer-learning techniques specifically designed for multi-fidelity problems, such as RUSH, to achieve further reductions in tuning time.",
    "Experiment Code": "class PASHARungSystem(PromotionRungSystem):\"\"\"Implements PASHA algorithm. It is very similar to ASHA, but it progressivelyextends the maximum resources if the ranking in the top two current rungs changes.A report introducing and evaluating the approach is available atTODO: add link\"\"\"    def __init__(        self,        rung_levels,        promote_quantiles,        metric,        mode,        resource_attr,        max_t,        ranking_criterion,        epsilon,        epsilon_scaling,    ):        super().__init__(            rung_levels, promote_quantiles, metric, mode, resource_attr, max_t        )        self.ranking_criterion = ranking_criterion        # define the index of the current top rung, starting from 1 for the lowest rung        #        self.current_rung_idx = 2        self.rung_levels = rung_levels        # initialize current maximum resources        self.current_max_t = rung_levels[self.current_rung_idx - 1]        self.epsilon = epsilon        self.epsilon_scaling = epsilon_scaling        if ranking_criterion == 'soft_ranking_auto':            self.per_epoch_results = {}            self.epoch_to_trials = {}            self.current_max_epoch = -1    # overriding the method in HB promotion to accomodate the increasing max resources level    def _effective_max_t(self):        return self.current_max_t    def _get_top_rungs_rankings(self, num_rungs=2):        \"\"\"        Look at the current top two rungs and get the rankings of the configurations.        The rungs can be empty, in which case we will return a list with 0 or 1 elements.        Normally the list will include rankings for both rungs.        The rankings are stored as a list of tuples (trial_id, rank, value).        Lower values have lower ranks, starting from zero. For example:        [('0', 0, 10.0), ('1', 3, 19.6), ('2', 2, 14.3), ('3', 1, 11.6)]        :param num_rungs: int describing how many top rungs to return        :return: rankings            List of at most two lists with tuple(trial_id, rank, score)        \"\"\"        rankings = []        # be careful, self._rungs is ordered with the highest resources level in the beginning        rungs = [self._rungs[-self.current_rung_idx + e] for e in range(num_rungs)]        for rung in rungs:            if rung.data != {}:                trial_ids = rung.data.keys()                values = []                for trial_id in trial_ids:                    values.append(rung.data[trial_id][0])                # order specifies where the value should be placed in the sorted list                values_order = np.array(values).argsort()                # calling argsort on the order will give us the ranking                values_ranking = values_order.argsort()                ranking = list(zip(trial_ids, values_ranking, values))                rankings.append(ranking)        return rankings    def _get_sorted_top_rungs(self, rankings):        \"\"\"        Sort the configurations in the top rung and the previous rung.        Filter out the configurations from the previous rung that        are not in the top rung.        :param rankings: list of at most two lists with tuple(trial_id, rank, score)        return: sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung        \"\"\"        # filter only the relevant configurations from the earlier rung        top_rung_keys = set([e[0] for e in rankings[0]])        corresponding_previous_rung_trials = filter(            lambda e: e[0] in top_rung_keys, rankings[1]        )        # if we try to maximize the objective, we need to reverse the ranking        if self._mode == \"max\":            reverse = True        else:            reverse = False        sorted_top_rung = sorted(rankings[0], key=lambda e: e[1], reverse=reverse)        sorted_previous_rung = sorted(            corresponding_previous_rung_trials, key=lambda e: e[1], reverse=reverse        )        return sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung    def _evaluate_soft_ranking(self, sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung) -> bool:        \"\"\"        Soft ranking creates groups of similarly performing configurations        and increases the resources only if a configuration goes outside of        its group.        :param sorted_top_rung: list of tuple(trial_id, rank, score)        :param sorted_previous_rung: list of tuple(sorted_top_rung, rank, score)        :return: keep_current_budget        \"\"\"        keep_current_budget = True        if len(sorted_previous_rung) < 2:            epsilon = 0.0        elif self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_std\":            epsilon = (                np.std([e[2] for e in sorted_previous_rung]) * self.epsilon_scaling            )        elif (            self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_median_dst\"            or self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_mean_dst\"        ):            scores = [e[2] for e in sorted_previous_rung]            distances = [                abs(e1 - e2)                for idx1, e1 in enumerate(scores)                for idx2, e2 in enumerate(scores)                if idx1 != idx2            ]            if self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_mean_dst\":                epsilon = np.mean(distances) * self.epsilon_scaling            elif self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_median_dst\":                epsilon = np.median(distances) * self.epsilon_scaling            else:                raise ValueError(                    \"Ranking criterion {} is not supported\".format(                        self.ranking_criterion                    )                )        else:            epsilon = self.epsilon        # create groups of configurations with similar performance        previous_rung_groups = []        for idx, item in enumerate(sorted_previous_rung):            current_rung_group = [item[0]]            # add configurations that are after the current configuration            for idx_after in range(idx + 1, len(sorted_previous_rung)):                new_item = sorted_previous_rung[idx_after]                if self._mode == \"max\":                    if new_item[2] < item[2] - epsilon:                        break                else:                    if new_item[2] > item[2] + epsilon:                        break                current_rung_group.append(new_item[0])            # add configurations that are before the current configuration            for idx_before in range(idx - 1, -1, -1):                new_item = sorted_previous_rung[idx_before]                if self._mode == \"max\":                    if new_item[2] > item[2] + epsilon:                        break                else:                    if new_item[2] < item[2] - epsilon:                        break                current_rung_group.append(new_item[0])            previous_rung_groups.append(set(current_rung_group))        # evaluate if a configuration has switched its group        for idx, item in enumerate(sorted_top_rung):            if item[0] not in previous_rung_groups[idx]:                keep_current_budget = False                break        return keep_current_budget    def _update_epsilon(self):        \"\"\"        This function is used to automatically calculate the value of epsilon.        It finds the configurations which swapped their rankings across rungs        and estimates the value of epsilon as the 90th percentile of the difference        between their performance in the previous rung.        The original value of epsilon is kept if no suitable configurations were found.        \"\"\"        seen_pairs = set()        noisy_cfg_distances = []        top_epoch = min(self.current_max_epoch, self._rungs[-self.current_rung_idx].level)        bottom_epoch = min(self._rungs[-self.current_rung_idx+1].level, self.current_max_epoch)        for epoch in range(top_epoch, bottom_epoch, -1):            if len(self.epoch_to_trials[epoch]) > 1:                for pair in itertools.combinations(self.epoch_to_trials[epoch], 2):                    c1, c2 = pair[0], pair[1]                    if (c1, c2) not in seen_pairs:                        seen_pairs.add((c1, c2))                        p1, p2 = self.per_epoch_results[c1][epoch], self.per_epoch_results[c2][epoch]                        cond = p1 > p2                        opposite_order = False                        same_order_after_opposite = False                        # now we need to check the earlier epochs to see if at any point they had a different order                        for prev_epoch in range(epoch - 1, 0, -1):                            pp1, pp2 = self.per_epoch_results[c1][prev_epoch], self.per_epoch_results[c2][prev_epoch]                            p_cond = pp1 > pp2                            if p_cond == (not cond):                                opposite_order = True                            if opposite_order and p_cond == cond:                                same_order_after_opposite = True                                break                        if opposite_order and same_order_after_opposite:                            noisy_cfg_distances.append(abs(p1 - p2))        if len(noisy_cfg_distances) > 0:            self.epsilon = np.percentile(noisy_cfg_distances, 90)            if str(self.epsilon) == 'nan':                raise ValueError('Epsilon became nan')     def _update_per_epoch_results(self, trial_id, result):        if trial_id not in self.per_epoch_results:            self.per_epoch_results[trial_id] = {}        self.per_epoch_results[trial_id][result[self._resource_attr]] = result[self._metric]        if result[self._resource_attr] not in self.epoch_to_trials:            self.epoch_to_trials[result[self._resource_attr]] = set()         self.epoch_to_trials[result[self._resource_attr]].add(trial_id)        if result[self._resource_attr] > self.current_max_epoch:            self.current_max_epoch = result[self._resource_attr]    def _decide_resource_increase(self, rankings) -> bool:        \"\"\"        Decide if to increase the resources given the current rankings.        Currently we look at the rankings and if elements in the first list        have the same order also in the second list, we keep the current resource        budget. If the rankings are different, we will increase the budget.        The rankings can only be incorrect if we have rankings for both rungs.        :param rankings: list of at most two lists with tuple(trial_id, rank, score)        return: not keep_current_budget        \"\"\"        if len(rankings) == 2:            sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung = self._get_sorted_top_rungs(rankings)        else:            return False        keep_current_budget = self._evaluate_soft_ranking(            sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung        )        return not keep_current_budget    def on_task_report(self, trial_id: str, result: dict, skip_rungs: int) -> dict:        \"\"\"        Apart from calling the superclass method, we also check the rankings        and decides if to increase the current maximum resources.        \"\"\"        ret_dict = super().on_task_report(trial_id, result, skip_rungs)        if self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_auto\":            self._update_per_epoch_results(trial_id, result)            self._update_epsilon()        # check the rankings and decide if to increase the current maximum resources        rankings = self._get_top_rungs_rankings(num_rungs=2)        increase_resources = self._decide_resource_increase(rankings)        # we have a maximum amount of resources that PASHA can use        # the resources should not increase indefinitely        if increase_resources:            if self.current_rung_idx < len(self._rungs):                self.current_rung_idx += 1                # be careful, self.rung_levels is ordered with the highest resources level at the end                # moreover, since we use rung levels for counting both from the beginning and from the end of the list                # we need to remember that counting from the beginning it's zero indexed                self.current_max_t = self.rung_levels[self.current_rung_idx - 1]            else:                self.current_max_t = self.max_t        return ret_dict",
    "Experiment Result": "PASHA is an extension of ASHA, implemented within the Syne Tune library as a `HyperbandScheduler` with `type='pasha'`. It introduces 'soft ranking,' where configurations are deemed equivalent if their performance difference is below an automatically estimated threshold, epsilon (ϵ). Epsilon is dynamically estimated by measuring noise in rankings, specifically by identifying pairs of configurations that repeatedly swap ranks across different resource levels. It is then set as the 90th percentile of performance differences for such 'criss-crossing' pairs when `ranking_criterion='soft_ranking_auto'`. Alternatively, epsilon can be manually set via `--pasha_epsilon`, or dynamically derived from other ranking criteria such as `soft_ranking_std`, `soft_ranking_median_dst`, or `soft_ranking_mean_dst`. The dynamically estimated epsilon can also be scaled using `--pasha_epsilon_scaling`. The algorithm starts with a small initial maximum resource allocation and progressively increases it only if the ranking of configurations in the top two rungs (promotion rounds) has not stabilized. PASHA uses a user-defined minimum resource (`grace_period`, default 1) and a reduction factor (`reduction_factor`, default 3) for pruning, and includes a maximum resource (`max_t`) as a safety net."

    "Title": "Hyperparameter Optimization through Neural Network Partitioning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes a novel and efficient hyperparameter optimization method called Partitioned Neural Networks, inspired by marginal likelihood. It allows for optimizing various hyperparameters (e.g., neural architecture, data augmentation, regularization, dropout rates, feature extractors) in a single training run without requiring a separate validation set, significantly reducing computational cost compared to traditional methods and other marginal likelihood approximations. A key contribution is its applicability and benefits in Federated Learning, where it enables better generalization with reduced communication overhead.",
    "Methodology": "The method partitions the training data into C data shards (D1, ..., DC) and the neural network model's parameters into C partitions (w1, ..., wC). Each parameter partition wk is associated with and optimized only on data from preceding shards D1:k. Subnetworks w(k)s are defined as concatenations of optimized parameters w1, ..., wk and fixed default values (e.g., initialization values) for subsequent partitions. The hyperparameter optimization objective (LML) is defined as the sum of 'out-of-training-sample' losses, i.e., the loss on data shard Dk using a subnetwork trained on D1:k-1. Training involves interleaving stochastic gradient updates for parameter partitions (on D1:k) and hyperparameters (on Dk using w(k-1)s). The method approximates a lower-bound on the marginal likelihood and uses standard backpropagation for gradients, avoiding Hessian computations.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on various tasks including toy input selection (model and differentiable), learning invariances through data augmentations, learning feature extractors, and federated learning. Datasets used include a synthetic toy input selection dataset, MNIST, CIFAR10, TinyImagenet, and their rotated variants (rotMNIST, rotCIFAR10, rotTinyImagenet), as well as subsets of these datasets for low-data regimes. Architectures varied from fully connected MLPs and a CNN (for MNIST) to fixupResNet-8, fixupResNet-14, ResNet-50, Wide ResNet-20, and ResNet-9 (for CIFAR10/TinyImagenet). Baselines included standard training (no augmentations), Augerino, Differentiable Laplace, Last-layer Marginal Likelihood, and FedAvg. Evaluation metrics primarily focused on test accuracy and log-likelihood.",
    "Limitations": "The method inherently requires an additional forward-backward pass for hyperparameter updates, incurring some computational overhead, though significantly less than existing marginal likelihood methods. It also tends to require more training iterations for convergence. Partitioning the network introduces constraints on its capacity, which can lead to some performance loss compared to optimally-tuned full, non-partitioned networks. Additionally, the method introduces new hyperparameters related to the partitioning strategy itself (number of chunks, proportions of data, and parameters per chunk), which might require tuning for optimal performance, despite observed robustness.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could explore alternative partitioning schemes that avoid the necessity for a separate forward-backward pass for parameters, potentially allowing for more efficient gradient computation. Further research could also investigate design choices such as gradient accumulation for hyperparameter updates and less frequent hyperparameter updates to reduce computational overhead. Opportunities for alleviating performance loss due to network partitioning, such as adjusting training rounds or increasing initial network capacity, are also suggested.",

    "Title": "Provably Efficient Online Hyperparameter Optimization with Population-Based Bandits",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces Population-Based Bandits (PB2), the first provably efficient PBT-style algorithm for online hyperparameter optimization. PB2 replaces the random heuristics of Population-Based Training (PBT) with theoretically guided Gaussian Process (GP) bandit optimization, allowing it to balance exploration and exploitation more effectively. A key contribution is the derivation of a sublinear regret bound for PB2, which quantifies the gap between optimal and achieved rewards, extending previous bandit results to a parallel setting. Experimentally, PB2 is shown to achieve high performance in reinforcement learning tasks with a modest computational budget, often outperforming PBT, especially with smaller population sizes and wider hyperparameter ranges.",
    "Methodology": "The online hyperparameter selection problem is formulated as batch Gaussian Process bandit optimization of a time-varying function, where maximizing final performance is equivalent to minimizing cumulative regret. PB2 uses a time-varying Gaussian Process as a surrogate model, incorporating a `ktime` kernel to represent the non-stationary nature of neural network training. For selecting hyperparameters for parallel agents, PB2 sequentially maximizes an acquisition function `µt,1(x) + √ βtσt,b(x)`, which updates uncertainty based on currently training agents to reduce redundancy. The algorithm periodically evaluates agents, copies weights from top performers for underperforming ones, and then uses the GP model to select new hyperparameters by maximizing this acquisition function. Unlike PBT, PB2 efficiently reuses data from previous trials.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments primarily focus on the Reinforcement Learning (RL) setting, known for its hyperparameter sensitivity. On-policy RL tasks include continuous control problems from OpenAI Gym (BipedalWalker, LunarLanderContinuous, Hopper, InvertedDoublePendulum), optimizing Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) hyperparameters (batch size, learning rate, GAE λ, PPO clip ϵ). Off-policy RL tasks include Breakout and SpaceInvaders from the Arcade Learning Environment, optimizing IMPALA hyperparameters (epsilon, learning rate, entropy coefficient). Population sizes of 4, 8, and 16 agents were evaluated. Baselines included Population Based Training (PBT), Random Search (RS), Bayesian Optimization (BO) using Expected Improvement, and ASHA. Experiments were run for 10^6 (PPO) or 10^7 (IMPALA) environment timesteps, with evaluations every 5 × 10^4 or 5 × 10^5 timesteps, respectively. Results were based on the median best reward or test accuracy across 10 (PPO), 7 (IMPALA), or 5 (CIFAR-10) random seeds, with interquartile ranges plotted. A supervised learning experiment on CIFAR-10 with a CNN was also conducted.",
    "Limitations": "The original PBT algorithm's main weaknesses include its reliance on random heuristics, leading to a lack of theoretical guarantees and a propensity for mode collapse to suboptimal regions, especially with small populations. PBT often requires vast computational resources and introduces new meta-hyperparameters that need tuning. Its greedy nature may lead to prematurely abandoning promising search space regions. Additionally, PBT's performance is sensitive to mis-specified or unknown hyperparameter ranges, as it relies on random sampling or gradual movements. While PB2 addresses these for PBT-style algorithms, the paper implies that its current integration with off-policy data or synthetic samples for RL is a future work.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include updating population sizes dynamically based on the acquisition function's value, and extending the search space beyond hyperparameters to also select optimization algorithms or neural network architectures using Bayesian Optimization. The authors also suggest exploring further gains in reinforcement learning experiments by making use of off-policy data or synthetic samples generated from learned dynamics models. Additionally, testing PB2's scalability with even larger population sizes and computational budgets is an interesting area for future work.",
    "Experiment Code": "from GPy.kern import Kern\nfrom GPy.core import Param\nfrom sklearn.metrics import pairwise_distances\nfrom sklearn.metrics.pairwise import euclidean_distances\n\nimport numpy as np\n\nclass TV_SquaredExp(Kern):\n    def __init__(self,input_dim, variance=1.,lengthscale=1.,epsilon=0.,active_dims=None):\n        super().__init__(input_dim, active_dims, 'time_se')\n        self.variance = Param('variance', variance)\n        self.lengthscale = Param('lengthscale', lengthscale)\n        self.epsilon = Param('epsilon', epsilon)\n        self.link_parameters(self.variance, self.lengthscale, self.epsilon)\n        \n    def K(self,X,X2):\n        # time must be in the far left column\n        if self.epsilon > 0.5: # 0.5\n            self.epsilon = 0.5\n        if X2 is None: X2 = np.copy(X)\n        T1 = X[:, 0].reshape(-1, 1)\n        T2 = X2[:, 0].reshape(-1, 1)\n        dists = pairwise_distances(T1,T2, 'cityblock')\n        timekernel=(1-self.epsilon)**(0.5*dists)\n        \n        X = X[:, 1:]\n        X2 = X2[:, 1:]\n\n        RBF = self.variance*np.exp(-np.square(euclidean_distances(X,X2))/self.lengthscale)\n        \n        return RBF * timekernel\n    \n    def Kdiag(self,X):\n        return self.variance*np.ones(X.shape[0])\n    \n    def update_gradients_full(self, dL_dK, X, X2):\n        if X2 is None: X2 = np.copy(X)\n        T1 = X[:, 0].reshape(-1, 1)\n        T2 = X2[:, 0].reshape(-1, 1)\n        \n        X = X[:, 1:]\n        X2 = X2[:, 1:]\n        dist2 = np.square(euclidean_distances(X,X2))/self.lengthscale\n    \n        dvar = np.exp(-np.square((euclidean_distances(X,X2))/self.lengthscale))\n        dl =  - (2 * euclidean_distances(X,X2)**2 * self.variance * np.exp(-dist2)) * self.lengthscale**(-2)\n        n = pairwise_distances(T1,T2, 'cityblock')/2\n        deps = -n * (1-self.epsilon)**(n-1)\n    \n        self.variance.gradient = np.sum(dvar*dL_dK)\n        self.lengthscale.gradient = np.sum(dl*dL_dK)\n        self.epsilon.gradient = np.sum(deps*dL_dK)\n",
    "Experiment Result": "The `TV_SquaredExp` kernel implements a time-varying squared exponential kernel. It takes `input_dim`, `variance`, `lengthscale`, and `epsilon` as parameters. The `K` method computes the kernel matrix as a product of a standard RBF kernel on `X[:, 1:]` and a time kernel `(1-epsilon)**(0.5*dists)` on `X[:, 0]` (time dimension), where `dists` are Cityblock distances. The `epsilon` parameter, representing the time decay, is clipped to `0.5` if it exceeds this value."

    "Title": "Differentiable Expected Hypervolume Improvement for Parallel Multi-Objective Bayesian Optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces q-Expected Hypervolume Improvement (qEHVI), a novel acquisition function for parallel, constrained multi-objective Bayesian optimization. Key contributions include: 1) A novel exact formulation of qEHVI (up to Monte-Carlo integration error) for parallel, constrained evaluation settings. 2) Computation of exact gradients for the MC estimator of qEHVI via auto-differentiation, enabling efficient optimization with first-order and quasi-second-order methods. 3) Leveraging Sample Average Approximation (SAA) for higher-order deterministic optimization with theoretical convergence guarantees. 4) Demonstration of qEHVI's computational tractability on modern GPU hardware, outperforming state-of-the-art MO BO algorithms in wall time and optimization performance. 5) Extension of qEHVI to support auxiliary outcome constraints and showing exact gradient computation for analytic EHVI for M > 2 objectives.",
    "Methodology": "The methodology involves deriving qEHVI by extending Expected Hypervolume Improvement (EHVI) to the parallel, constrained setting. It utilizes box decompositions and the inclusion-exclusion principle to compute the joint Hypervolume Improvement (HVI) of 'q' new points. The expected value (EHVI) is then estimated via Monte-Carlo (MC) integration using samples from the joint posterior of a Gaussian Process (GP) surrogate model. Exact gradients of the MC estimator are computed using auto-differentiation and the re-parameterization trick. For optimization, the Sample Average Approximation (SAA) approach is employed, allowing for deterministic, higher-order optimizers. Outcome constraints are incorporated by feasibility-weighting on the sample-level, with a differentiable sigmoid approximation replacing the indicator function for differentiability. The formulation is designed for embarrassing parallelism.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The evaluation was conducted on a mix of synthetic and real-world problems. Synthetic benchmarks include Branin-Currin (d=2, M=2), DTLZ2 (d=6, M=2), and the constrained C2-DTLZ2 (d=12, M=2, V=1), with additional tests on noisy Branin-Currin and DTLZ2 (M=3, M=4). Real-world problems include Structural Optimization in Automobile Safety Design (5 reinforced parts, 3 objectives) and Policy Optimization for Adaptive Bitrate Control (4 parameters, 2 objectives). Performance was measured by log hypervolume difference or hypervolume indicator. All methods used independent Gaussian Processes with Matern 5/2 ARD kernels. Baselines included SMS-EGO, PESMO, TS-TCH, analytic EHVI, qPAREGO (a novel parallel/constrained extension), and a quasi-random approach. Optimization of acquisition functions used L-BFGS-B; SMS-EGO and PESMO used finite difference gradients, while others (including qEHVI) used exact gradients. Initial designs had 2(d+1) points from a scrambled Sobol sequence. qPAREGO and qEHVI used N=128 QMC samples. Experiments were run on CPU (2x Intel Xeon E5-2680 v4) and GPU (Tesla V100-SXM2-16GB).",
    "Limitations": "The primary limitations of the proposed qEHVI approach are: 1) It currently assumes noiseless observations, a common constraint across all existing EHVI formulations. 2) Its scalability is limited by the underlying partitioning algorithm used for hypervolume computation, making its direct use in very high-dimensional objective spaces (e.g., M ≥ 4) computationally challenging. While compatible with approximate partitioning algorithms, this introduces approximation error.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include: 1) Extending qEHVI to account for noisy observations by integrating over the uncertainty around previous observations, potentially using Monte-Carlo samples over new candidates and training points. 2) Enhancing scalability in high-dimensional objective spaces by integrating more scalable partitioning algorithms, whether approximate (e.g., Couckuyt et al. [11]) or more efficient exact algorithms (e.g., [41, 17, 69]), which could reduce memory footprint and computation time. 3) Deriving a formal convergence rate for the optimizer when using the SAA approach. 4) Further extending the theoretical results to situations where randomized Quasi-Monte Carlo methods are used for base samples. 5) Generally encouraging the application of modern computational paradigms and tooling to Bayesian optimization to foster further advancements.",
    "Experiment Code": "import torch\nfrom botorch.acquisition.multi_objective.base import MultiObjectiveMCAcquisitionFunction\nfrom botorch.acquisition.multi_objective.objective import    eta: Tensor | float = 1e-3,\n        fat: bool = False,\n    ) -> None:\n        legacy_ei_numerics_warning(legacy_name=type(self).__name__)\n        if len(ref_point) != dtype=partitioning.pareto_Y.dtype,\n            device=partitioning.pareto_Y.device,\n        )\n        super().__init__(\n            model=model,\n            sampler=sampler,\n            objective=objective,\n            constraints=constraints,\n            eta=eta,\n            X_pending=X_pending,\n        )\n        self.register_buffer(\"ref_point\", ref_point)\n        cell_bounds = partitioning.get_hypercell_bounds()\n        self.register_buffer(\"cell_lower_bounds\", cell_bounds[0])\n        self.register_buffer(\"cell_upper_bounds\", cell_bounds[1])\n        SubsetIndexCachingMixin.__init__(self)\n        self.fat = fat\n\n    def _compute_qehvi(self, samples: Tensor, X: Tensor | None = None) -> Tensor:\n        obj = self.objective(samples, X=X)\n        q = obj.shape[-2]\n        if self.constraints is not None:\n            feas_weights = compute_smoothed_feasibility_indicator(\n                constraints=self.constraints,\n                samples=samples,\n                eta=self.eta,\n                fat=self.fat,\n            )\n        device = self.ref_point.device\n        q_subset_indices = self.compute_q_subset_indices(q_out=q, device=device)\n        batch_shape = obj.shape[:-2]\n        areas_per_segment = torch.zeros(\n            *batch_shape,\n            self.cell_lower_bounds.shape[-2],\n            dtype=obj.dtype,\n            device=device,\n        )\n        cell_batch_ndim = self.cell_lower_bounds.ndim - 2\n        sample_batch_view_shape = torch.Size(\n            [\n                batch_shape[0] if cell_batch_ndim > 0 else 1,\n                *[1 for _ in range(len(batch_shape) - max(cell_batch_ndim, 1))],\n                *self.cell_lower_bounds.shape[1:-2],\n            ]\n        )\n        view_shape = (\n            *sample_batch_view_shape,\n            self.cell_upper_bounds.shape[-2],\n            1,\n            self.cell_upper_bounds.shape[-1],\n        )\n        for i in range(1, self.q_out + 1):\n            q_choose_i = q_subset_indices[f\"q_choose_{i}\"]\n            obj_subsets = obj.index_select(dim=-2, index=q_choose_i.view(-1))\n            obj_subsets = obj_subsets.view(\n                obj.shape[:-2] + q_choose_i.shape + obj.shape[-1:]\n            )\n            overlap_vertices = obj_subsets.min(dim=-2).values\n            overlap_vertices = torch.min(\n                overlap_vertices.unsqueeze(-3), self.cell_upper_bounds.view(view_shape)\n            )\n            lengths_i = (\n                overlap_vertices - self.cell_lower_bounds.view(view_shape)\n            ).clamp_min(0.0)\n            areas_i = lengths_i.prod(dim=-1)\n            if self.constraints is not None:\n                feas_subsets = feas_weights.index_select(\n                    dim=-1, index=q_choose_i.view(-1)\n                ).view(feas_weights.shape[:-1] + q_choose_i.shape)\n                areas_i = areas_i * feas_subsets.unsqueeze(-3).prod(dim=-1)\n            areas_i = areas_i.sum(dim=-1)\n            areas_per_segment += (-1) ** (i + 1) * areas_i\n        return areas_per_segment.sum(dim=-1).mean(dim=0)\n\n    @concatenate_pending_points\n    @t_batch_mode_transform()\n    @average_over_ensemble_models\n    def forward(self, X: Tensor) -> Tensor:\n        posterior = self.model.posterior(X)\n        samples = self.get_posterior_samples(posterior)\n        return self._compute_qehvi(samples=samples, X=X)",
    "Experiment Result": "The methodology derives qEHVI by extending Expected Hypervolume Improvement (EHVI) to a parallel, constrained setting.\n\n**Key Implementation Details:**\n- **Joint HVI Computation:** Utilizes box decompositions (specifically, `NondominatedPartitioning`) and the inclusion-exclusion principle to compute the joint Hypervolume Improvement (HVI) for `q` new points.\n- **EHVI Estimation:** The expected value is estimated via Monte-Carlo (MC) integration. Samples are drawn from the joint posterior of a Gaussian Process (GP) surrogate model.\n- **Gradients:** Exact gradients of the MC estimator are computed using auto-differentiation and the re-parameterization trick. This is achieved through the differentiable nature of the entire computation graph.\n- **Optimization Approach:** The Sample Average Approximation (SAA) approach is inherently supported, as the acquisition function returns a deterministic value by averaging over MC samples, enabling the use of deterministic, higher-order optimizers.\n- **Outcome Constraints:** Constraints are incorporated by feasibility-weighting at the sample level. A differentiable sigmoid approximation (`compute_smoothed_feasibility_indicator`) is used to replace the indicator function for differentiability. The `eta` parameter controls the smoothness of this approximation, and `fat` parameter toggles the logarithmic/linear asymptotic behavior.\n- **Parallelism:** The formulation is designed for embarrassing parallelism, handling `X_pending` (pending design points) by concatenating them with the current batch of candidates for joint evaluation.\n\n**Experimental Settings & Parameters:**\n- `ref_point`: A list or tensor representing the reference point (in the outcome space) against which hypervolume is computed.\n- `partitioning`: A `NondominatedPartitioning` module, providing the non-dominated front and a partitioning of the non-dominated space into hyper-rectangles. If constraints are present, this partitioning includes only feasible points.\n- `sampler`: An `MCSampler` is used for drawing base samples for MC integration. The default sample shape (number of MC samples) is typically 512.\n- `objective`: An `MCMultiOutputObjective` defining how multi-output samples are evaluated. Defaults to `IdentityMCMultiOutputObjective()` if not specified.\n- `constraints`: A list of callable functions, where negative values imply feasibility. These are used for feasibility-weighting.\n- `eta`: Temperature parameter(s) (float or tensor) for the sigmoid approximation of constraint indicators, typically `1e-3`.\n- `fat`: Boolean flag indicating whether to use a heavy-tailed approximation for the constraint indicator, default `False`.\n- `alpha`: A hyperparameter for `NondominatedPartitioning` controlling approximation level (default 0.0 for exact partitioning for <=4 objectives, increasing for more).\n- `Y`: Observed outcomes required to initialize the `NondominatedPartitioning` (e.g., to determine the current Pareto front).\n\n**Reference:**\n- [Daulton2020qehvi] Differentiable Expected Hypervolume Improvement for Parallel Multi-Objective Bayesian Optimization. Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33, 2020."

    "Title": "Gradient Descent: The Ultimate Optimizer",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces a novel method to automatically compute hypergradients with a simple modification to backpropagation, enabling gradient-based optimizers (like SGD and Adam) to tune their own hyperparameters. Key contributions include eliminating the need for manual differentiation of optimizer update rules, allowing optimization of all hyperparameters (not just learning rates, e.g., momentum coefficients), and demonstrating the practical implementation of recursively stacked higher-order hyperoptimizers. These 'towers of optimizers' are shown to become increasingly robust and less sensitive to the initial human-chosen hyperparameter values, validating a previously hypothesized effect.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves replacing manual differentiation of optimizer update rules with Automatic Differentiation (AD) for computing hypergradients. The key insight is to modify the standard reverse-mode AD process by carefully managing the computation graph: while model weights are detached at each optimization step to prevent quadratic-time complexity, hyperparameters (like learning rates) are *not* detached, allowing backpropagation to compute gradients with respect to them. This enables continuous, on-line adaptation of hyperparameters. The method generalizes easily to various optimizers (Adam, AdaGrad, RMSProp) and allows simultaneous optimization of all their hyperparameters (e.g., alpha, beta1, beta2 for Adam), with minor adjustments like clamping values to valid domains and special initialization for Adam to avoid division by zero. Recursion is achieved by refactoring the hyperoptimizer to take another optimizer as a parameter, allowing arbitrarily tall stacks of hyperoptimizers, where higher-level optimizers tune the hyperparameters of the layers below them.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on a single NVIDIA TITAN Xp GPU. Initial validation involved an MLP with one fully-connected hidden layer (128 nodes, tanh activations) on the MNIST dataset, trained for 30 epochs with a batch size of 256. This setup was used to test hyperoptimized SGD, Adam, AdaGrad, and RMSProp, comparing against their regular counterparts and re-running regular optimizers with learned hyperparameters. For larger-scale evaluation, ResNet-20 was trained on the CIFAR-10 dataset for 200-500 epochs to assess robustness to 'bad' initial learning rate and momentum choices, and to compare against hand-engineered learning rate decay schedules. A character-level RNN (2-layer LSTM with 128 hidden nodes) was trained on the Tolstoy dataset for 50,000 gradient descent steps to evaluate performance on language modeling with varying initial learning rates. Higher-order hyperoptimization was tested across all three benchmarks (MLP on MNIST, CNN on CIFAR-10, RNN on Tolstoy) with increasing stack heights and bottom-level step sizes initialized across many orders of magnitude, with a specific initialization scheme for higher layers. A final scalability test involved fine-tuning a pre-trained ResNet-152 on ImageNet to the Caltech-256 dataset.",
    "Limitations": "One limitation is the instability and divergence of the system when initial hyperparameters are set 'far too high,' preventing the hyperoptimizer from having an effect. The current implementation also requires careful computation graph management to avoid bugs, such as loggers needing to detach logged tensors to prevent memory leaks. Furthermore, certain built-in PyTorch modules (e.g., LSTM) cannot be directly used because they silently modify the computation graph, potentially leading to incorrect gradients with this system.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include designing hyperoptimizers that are robust to 'far too high' initial hyperparameters, which would likely require a deeper theoretical analysis of convergence. Another direction is to design differentiable programming languages that allow methods like this to be expressed in a more modular and composable manner, minimizing the risk of computation graph-related bugs. The authors also hope that advances in on-line hyperparameter tuning can help reduce the significant energy impact of hyperparameter searches in deep learning.",

    "Title": "Stochastic Marginal Likelihood Gradients using Neural Tangent Kernels",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the challenge of hyperparameter optimization in deep learning, particularly the scalability issues associated with using marginal likelihood gradients, which traditionally require processing the entire dataset. It introduces novel lower bounds to the linearized Laplace approximation of the marginal likelihood, which are amenable to stochastic-gradient-based optimization. These bounds allow for batched computation and offer a flexible trade-off between estimation accuracy and computational complexity. By leveraging the function-space form of the linearized Laplace approximation and estimating it using the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK), the authors demonstrate significant acceleration (up to 25-fold) of gradient-based hyperparameter optimization, achieving performance comparable to or superior to previous full-batch methods, especially for larger datasets.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology revolves around approximating the marginal likelihood using the linearized Laplace approximation and enabling its stochastic optimization. The key techniques include: 1. **Parametric Structured Lower Bounds**: Demonstrating (Theorem 1) that existing structured approximations of the Generalized Gauss-Newton (GGN) Hessian (e.g., block-diagonal or diagonal variants like KFAC) are valid lower bounds to the Laplace log marginal likelihood. 2. **Subset-of-Data Kernel Bounds**: Deriving novel lower bounds (Theorem 2) by using the dual NTK form of the log-determinant, which is typically intractable for stochastic gradients. This involves partitioning the input-output index set into subsets, leading to a block-diagonal NTK approximation that permits unbiased stochastic estimates and gradients. 3. **Parametric Data Subset Bounds**: Proving an equivalence (Theorem 3) between the NTK-based subset bound and a parametric variant employing a GGN computed on data subsets. 4. **Parametric Doubly Lower Bound**: Combining the parametric structured bound and the data subset bound (Corollary 3.1) to allow structured parametric approximations (e.g., KFAC) to be used with stochastic optimization on data subsets. The paper also explores strategies for tighter bounds, such as output-wise partitioning (processing single outputs per batch) and grouping inputs by class labels. An interleaved optimization algorithm is used, where network weights and hyperparameters are updated with stochastic gradient steps.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The research employs a multi-faceted experimental setup: 1. **Illustrative Examples**: Uses a small 3-layer Convolutional Neural Network (~16,000 parameters) on random subsets of 1,000 MNIST digits, including fully rotated MNIST, to optimize scalar or layer-wise prior precision (weight decay) and rotational invariance. This setup allows for comparison against the exact linearized Laplace for reference and analysis of bound tightness, test log-likelihood, and test accuracy, as well as Pareto-efficiency (runtime vs. tightness). 2. **Large-Scale Benchmarks**: Evaluates the proposed estimators on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 using a Wide ResNet 16-4, and on TinyImagenet (100,000 data points, 200 classes) with a ResNet-50 (~23 million parameters, modified with Fixup initialization). Hyperparameters optimized include layer-wise prior precisions and affine invariances. 3. **Comparative Analysis**: Compares the stochastic estimators (e.g., NTK-150-1, KFAC-60-1, often using single-output approximations) against state-of-the-art full-batch methods like KFAC-Laplace, and other baselines such as Augerino and network partitioning. Evaluation metrics include log marginal likelihood estimates, test log-likelihood, test accuracy, and practical runtime speed-ups.",
    "Limitations": "The inherent limitation of previous approaches is that the log-determinant term in the standard linearized Laplace approximation does not allow for unbiased stochastic gradients over data batches, necessitating the development of new lower bounds. The paper notes that using excessively small subset sizes for stochastic estimators can lead to optimization failures. While empirical results show strong performance, theoretically, for larger datasets, maintaining constant subset sizes might lead to looser bounds. Additionally, class-wise partitioning, while sometimes improving bounds, was observed to incur a larger variance in the estimated bound. Scalability challenges are evident with full-batch methods like KFAC-N-C, which are shown to be intractable and run out of memory for datasets with many classes such as CIFAR-100 and TinyImagenet, underscoring the necessity and advantage of the proposed stochastic estimators.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could focus on several promising avenues. One direction is to develop advanced methods for making the derived bounds even tighter, potentially by improving the partitioning strategies of the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK). Another area involves exploring techniques to optimally group input-output pairs during training, possibly by tracking (anti-)correlations between inputs in the NTK, drawing inspiration from methods like inducing point optimization in Gaussian Processes. Furthermore, the paper suggests applying the developed fast stochastic estimators to large-scale Bayesian linear models to assess their utility in that context. Lastly, the efficiency of these estimators opens opportunities for learning more complex and diverse hyperparameters with Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD), beyond the prior precision and invariances explored in this work.",

    "Title": "Implicit differentiation of Lasso-type models for hyperparameter optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the challenge of setting regularization parameters for Lasso-type estimators, which is crucial but difficult. Existing gradient-based hyperparameter optimization (HO) methods suffer from high memory consumption (forward/backward automatic differentiation) or numerical instability/prohibitive cost for non-smooth problems (standard implicit differentiation). This work introduces an efficient implicit differentiation algorithm, without matrix inversion, specifically designed for Lasso-type problems. The main contributions are: 1) Demonstrating that forward iterative differentiation of block coordinate descent (BCD), a state-of-the-art solver for Lasso-type problems, converges linearly towards the true gradient once the support is identified. 2) Proposing an algorithm (Implicit Forward Iterative Differentiation, Algorithm 2) that decouples Jacobian computation from regression coefficient computation, avoiding linear system inversion and scaling to high-dimensional data by leveraging solution sparsity. 3) Showing through extensive benchmarks that the proposed method outperforms a large number of standard HO methods in optimizing error on held-out data or the Stein Unbiased Risk Estimator (SURE).",
    "Methodology": "The methodology casts hyperparameter optimization as a bi-level optimization problem. For non-smooth Lasso-type problems, it leverages the fixed-point iteration property of proximal BCD algorithms (such as ISTA) to derive weak Jacobians. The core technique is 'Implicit Forward Iterative Differentiation' (Algorithm 2). This method first computes the regression coefficients using a standard Lasso solver and identifies the support. Then, it applies forward iterative differentiation restricted to the identified support, which leads to efficient and stable Jacobian computation without solving large, potentially ill-conditioned linear systems (as in classical implicit differentiation, Algorithm 1) or storing all intermediate iterates (as in backward iterative differentiation). The method relies on Proposition 1, which provides closed-form, sparse Jacobian solutions for Lasso and weighted Lasso, and Proposition 2, which proves the linear convergence of the Jacobian sequence once the support is identified.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The Python code is released as an open-source package 'sparse-ho', with critical parts implemented using Numba. For fair comparison, all methods used the same vanilla BCD algorithm (Algorithm 5) for the inner optimization problem, with a stopping criterion of `(f(β(k+1))-f(β(k)))/f(0) < ϵtol = 10^-5`. Hypergradient-based methods employed a line-search strategy, and initialization was set to `λmax - log(10)`. Competitors included gradient-based methods (Proposed Implicit Forward Iterdiff, Implicit differentiation, Forward Iterdiff) and non-gradient-based methods (Grid-search, Random-search, Lattice Hypercube sampling, Bayesian optimization). Experiments were conducted on: 1) Held-out loss for Lasso on real-world, high-dimensional datasets: `rcv1` (n=20242, p=19959), `20news` (n=11314, p=130107), and `finance` (n=16087, p=1668737), splitting data into train, validation, and test sets. 2) Held-out loss for the non-convex MCP estimator on `rcv1` and `20news`. 3) SURE criterion for Lasso and weighted Lasso on synthetic data, with `n=100`, varying `p` from 200 to 10,000, 5 non-zero coefficients in `β*`, and SNR=3. Evaluation metrics included validation/test loss and normalized Mean Squared Error (MSE) for estimation performance. A specific toy example was used to test performance in cases of non-unique inner problem solutions.",
    "Limitations": "The theoretical framework developed in this work does not cover non-convex cases, although numerical experiments for the non-convex MCP estimator demonstrate proper behavior. The overall hyperparameter optimization problem (L) is generally non-convex, meaning convergence to a global minimum is not guaranteed. While implicit differentiation is made more efficient, classical implicit differentiation (Algorithm 1) can still suffer from slow convergence due to ill-conditioned linear systems. Furthermore, in pathological cases where the Lasso solution is not unique, the mapping from hyperparameters to regression coefficients (λ↦→β̂(λ)) might not be continuous, theoretically limiting gradient-based methods, though the proposed algorithm can still be applied without theoretical guarantees. When using the SURE criterion, knowledge of the noise variance is required, which can be a practical limitation.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Handling non-convex penalty cases (e.g., MCP) for theoretical guarantees is explicitly mentioned as a direction for future work. The paper also implicitly suggests that further research could involve extending the approach to other regularization formulations like Elastic-Net.",

    "Title": "AUTOMATA: Gradient Based Data Subset Selection for Compute-Efficient Hyper-parameter Tuning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes AUTOMATA, a gradient-based subset selection framework for compute-efficient hyper-parameter tuning. The core idea is to use small, informative data subsets (1%-30% of the full dataset) for model training runs during hyper-parameter optimization, significantly accelerating the tuning process. AUTOMATA achieves speedups of 3x-30x with minimal performance loss (0%-2% relative test error) compared to tuning with the entire dataset, thereby also reducing energy consumption and CO2 emissions. It is the first work to systematically study the role of intelligent data subset selection for hyper-parameter tuning, demonstrating its effectiveness when combined with existing hyper-parameter search and scheduling algorithms.",
    "Methodology": "AUTOMATA integrates three main components: a hyper-parameter search algorithm (e.g., Random Search, TPE), a gradient-based subset selection (GSS) algorithm for efficient configuration evaluation, and a hyper-parameter scheduling algorithm (e.g., Hyperband, ASHA). For GSS, AUTOMATA selects a subset and associated weights such that the weighted subset loss gradient approximates the entire training loss gradient, formulated as an optimization problem (Equation 1). This is solved using a greedy algorithm like Orthogonal Matching Pursuit (OMP). A 'per-batch subset selection' variant is used for efficiency, where mini-batches are selected instead of individual data points. Model warm-starting is employed for ASHA scheduler experiments to ensure informative loss gradients early in training. The framework can incorporate any existing search and scheduling algorithms.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The effectiveness of AUTOMATA was empirically evaluated on real-world datasets spanning three domains: text (SST2, SST5, glue-SST2, TREC6), image (CIFAR10, CIFAR100, SVHN), and tabular (DNA, SATIMAGE, LETTER, CONNECT-4). For text datasets, LSTM models with GloVe embeddings were used; for image datasets, ResNet18 and ResNet50 models; and for tabular datasets, a multi-layer perceptron with 2 hidden layers. Experiments were conducted using combinations of hyper-parameter search algorithms (Random Search, TPE) and scheduling algorithms (Hyperband, ASHA). Baselines included RANDOM (randomly sampled subsets), CRAIG (a gradient-based subset selection method for efficient supervised learning), and FULL (using the entire dataset). Subset size fractions of 1%, 5%, 10%, and 30% were tested. Evaluation metrics included speedup vs. relative test error, energy consumption, and CO2 emissions. Experiments were repeated 3-5 times to report mean accuracy and speedups.",
    "Limitations": "One limitation of AUTOMATA is the uncertainty regarding the minimum subset size required to achieve desired speedups without performance loss. In scenarios where no performance degradation is acceptable, the framework currently relies on larger subset sizes (e.g., 10%, 30%). This suggests a potential dependency on manually pre-defined subset sizes rather than dynamically determined optimal ones.",
    "Future Research Directions": "A key future research direction is to adaptively change subset sizes based on model performance for each configuration. This would remove the current dependency on fixed subset sizes and potentially allow for more granular control over the speed-accuracy trade-off, especially in scenarios aiming for zero performance loss.",

Output:
{
    "open_problems": "AUTOMATA shows that replacing full-data training runs by runs on small, informative subsets (e.g. 5 – 30 %) accelerates hyper-parameter search with little loss.  However, the subset size is fixed by the user.  • Too small → ranking of configurations becomes noisy and the optimiser discards good candidates.  • Too large → unnecessary computation.  A simple, automatic rule for adapting the subset fraction during the search is missing.",
    "methods": "Progressive-AUTOMATA (P-AUTOMATA).\nTake any AUTOMATA set–up (search + scheduler + GradMatchPB subsetter) and replace the fixed subset fraction f with a progressive schedule that increases f only when necessary.\n1. Initial fraction f ← f0  (e.g. 0.05).\n2. After each scheduler rung (i.e. all configurations have been evaluated once at the current fidelity) compute the ranking R_t of the top k configurations by validation accuracy.\n3. Measure ranking instability  Δ_t = 1 – |R_t ∩ R_{t-1}| / k  ( Jaccard complement between two successive rungs; Δ_t∈[0,1] ).\n4. If  Δ_t > ϵ  (e.g. ϵ = 0.3) increase the subset size  f ← min(γf, f_max) with γ = 2.\n5. Keep GradMatchPB unchanged but pass the current f.\n\nTheoretical intuition: The subset gradients approximate full-data gradients with error that drops as O(1/√n).  If rankings are unstable the variance term dominates; doubling n halves the standard error, giving a cheap adaptive bias–variance trade-off.  When rankings stabilise we stop growing f, saving compute.",
    "experimental_setup": "Base method: AUTOMATA + ASHA + Random Search, fixed f = 0.1.\nProposed: P-AUTOMATA with f0 = 0.05, γ = 2, ϵ = 0.3, f_max = 0.3.\nDatasets & models (single-GPU):\n• CIFAR-10 with ResNet-18 (same as paper).\n• SST-2 with 1-layer LSTM (same as paper).\nHyper-parameter space exactly as AUTOMATA (lr, hidden_size, batch_size…).\nBudget: 50 configurations, ASHA (η = 3) until 30 epochs (image) / 10 epochs (text).\nMetrics: (1) Best validation accuracy, (2) Wall-clock time, (3) Test accuracy of best config.\nRun 3 seeds and report mean ± std.",
    "experimental_code": "import time, copy, random\nimport torch\nfrom automata import GradMatchPB   # assumes AUTOMATA code base\n\ndef progressive_fraction(prev_rank, curr_rank, f, eps=0.3, gamma=2, f_max=0.3):\n    if not prev_rank:  # first rung\n        return f, curr_rank\n    k = len(curr_rank)\n    overlap = len(set(prev_rank) & set(curr_rank))\n    delta = 1 - overlap / k\n    if delta > eps:\n        f = min(gamma * f, f_max)\n    return f, curr_rank\n\n# pseudocode for one ASHA rung\nsubset_frac = 0.05\nprev_top = []\nfor rung in range(num_rungs):\n    top_scores = []\n    for cfg in rung_candidates:  # supplied by ASHA\n        subset = GradMatchPB(dataset, fraction=subset_frac, select_every=1)\n        model = build_model(cfg)\n        train(model, subset)\n        val_acc = evaluate(model, val_loader)\n        top_scores.append((val_acc, cfg))\n    # keep k best configs\n    top_scores.sort(reverse=True)\n    curr_top = [c for _, c in top_scores[:k]]\n    subset_frac, prev_top = progressive_fraction(prev_top, curr_top, subset_frac)\n",
    "expected_result": "• On CIFAR-10 the adaptive run is expected to start with f ≈ 0.05, rise to ≈0.2 by the second rung, and match the validation accuracy of fixed-0.1 AUTOMATA within 98-100 % while saving ~30 % total GPU time.\n• On SST-2 rankings typically stabilise early; f stops at 0.1 yielding identical accuracy to the baseline but ~40 % faster.\nAcross seeds the variance of validation accuracy is comparable to baseline, indicating the stability criterion prevents excessive noise.",
    "expected_conclusion": "A single, three-line heuristic that monitors configuration-ranking stability removes the need to hand-pick the subset fraction in AUTOMATA.  The Progressive-AUTOMATA variant preserves accuracy while cutting additional compute when small subsets suffice, and enlarging them only when gradient noise harms the search.  This minimal change makes subset-based HPO robust and completely self-tuning, further lowering the practical barrier to efficient hyper-parameter optimisation."
